# IsoDoom — Specification

An isometric, top-down remake of the classic *Doom*. It uses the original game data loaded at runtime from the player's own IWAD, much as [Isowulf](https://www.moddb.com/mods/isowulf) does for Wolfenstein 3D.

Status: draft v0.1 (2026-10-05)

---

## 1. Goals

- Play the original Doom maps from a fixed isometric camera, Diablo/Isowulf style.
- Use **only** the original assets (maps, textures, flats, sprites, sounds, music, palette, UI graphics), read from the WAD at runtime. No WAD content is shipped or converted ahead of time.
- Keep the original game's feel: same monsters, weapons, damage, AI behaviour, and map specials (doors, lifts, crushers, switches, teleporters, secrets, keys). Change things only where the camera makes it necessary (see §6.3).
- Use twin-stick controls: move with WASD, aim with the mouse.

### Non-goals (for v1)

- Shipping multiplayer or co-op in v1. Online co-op and deathmatch are milestone M11 (§11, `docs/plans/multiplayer.md`), so the sim is built deterministic from day one (see §6.1). If that becomes a significant burden during development, the decision will be revisited.
- Boom, MBF, UDMF, ZDoom or other extended map formats, and DeHackEd patches. Vanilla format only (§5.3 weighs each one for after v1).
- Exact demo (.lmp) compatibility with vanilla Doom.
- Source-port extras such as jumping, crouching or freelook.

## 2. Target content (phased)

| Phase | IWAD | Notes |
|---|---|---|
| 1 | `DOOM1.WAD` (shareware, E1M1–E1M9) | Development baseline |
| 2 | `DOOM.WAD` (registered / Ultimate Doom, E1–E4) | Adds episode select, E2–E4 bosses, the Spider Mastermind and Cyberdemon |
| 3 | `DOOM2.WAD` (and later `TNT.WAD`, `PLUTONIA.WAD`) | MAPxx naming, Super Shotgun, new monsters, Icon of Sin, Doom II texture and intermission differences |
| Later | Vanilla-format PWADs, Freedoom | Load order IWAD + PWADs |

The game identifies the IWAD from its lumps (`E1M1` without `E2M1` means shareware, `MAP01` means Doom II, and so on) and enables the matching game mode, in the same way vanilla's `IdentifyVersion` does.

## 3. Platform & technology

- **Engine:** Godot 4.x, Forward+ (Compatibility renderer as a fallback).
- **Language:** C# (.NET 10, LTS) is the recommendation. Porting C game logic, doing fixed-point maths and parsing binary WADs are much more comfortable and faster in C# than in GDScript. GDScript stays available for UI and glue code. *Trade-off:* Godot 4 C# projects cannot currently export to the web (see §12).
- **Targets:** desktop only, with **Linux x86_64 as a first-class target, including the Steam Deck**. Windows is also supported, and macOS is best-effort. There is no web export.
  - Steam Deck: runs as a native Linux build (no Proton needed). Designed for 1280×800 (16:10). The UI and HUD are fully gamepad-navigable, and text and HUD are readable on a 7" screen.
  - The CI builds and smoke-tests the Linux export on every change, and the Windows export (T8.4) and the macOS export (T8.4a) on their own runners.
- **License:** the gameplay simulation is ported from the id Software Doom source (GPL-2.0), so the project is **GPL-2.0-or-later**. Only code is distributed, never WAD data.
- **Shareware download:** if no IWAD is found, the game offers to download the shareware `DOOM1.WAD` v1.9 (the shareware license allows unmodified redistribution), as Isowulf does. It is verified by hash before use. *Done in T1.1b (§12):* the official `doom19s.zip`'s two installer parts, joined, are a PKZIP self-extractor that .NET's `ZipArchive` reads, so it was cheap.

## 4. Architecture overview

```
 ┌──────────────┐   ┌───────────────────┐   ┌────────────────────┐
 │  WAD layer   │──▶│  Asset decoders   │──▶│  Godot resources   │
 │ (lump dir,   │   │ palette, patches, │   │ Textures, atlases, │
 │  PWAD merge) │   │ flats, sprites,   │   │ AudioStreams,      │
 └──────────────┘   │ sounds, MUS→MIDI, │   │ materials          │
                    │ map lumps         │   └─────────┬──────────┘
                    └─────────┬─────────┘             │
                              ▼                       ▼
                    ┌───────────────────┐   ┌────────────────────┐
                    │  Simulation       │──▶│  Presentation      │
                    │  35 Hz tic loop,  │   │  Level mesh, sprite│
                    │  fixed-point,     │   │  billboards, iso   │
                    │  port of p_*.c    │   │  camera, cutaway,  │
                    │  (pure C#, no     │   │  HUD, menus, audio │
                    │   Godot types)    │   │  (interpolated)    │
                    └───────────────────┘   └────────────────────┘
```

Key rule: **the simulation does not depend on Godot.** It is a plain C# library that takes input commands (`ticcmd`) and produces world state. The presentation layer reads that state each frame and interpolates between tics. This keeps the sim testable and deterministic, and leaves room for networking later.

### 4.1 Proposed project layout

```
isodoom/
  project.godot
  src/
    Wad/           # WAD reader, lump directory, IWAD detection
    Assets/        # palette, colormap, patch/flat/texture composition, sprites, sounds, music
    Sim/           # deterministic game logic (port of p_*, info.c, m_random, tables)
    Map/           # map lump parsing, BSP, blockmap, reject, sector polygon building
    Net/           # the lockstep protocol: messages, lobby, NetServer/NetClient, transports (M11)
    Render/        # level mesh builder, materials/shaders, sprites, camera, cutaway
    Game/          # game flow: menus, intermission, finale, save/load, options
    Audio/
  shaders/
  scenes/
  tests/           # GdUnit4 / xUnit tests for Wad, Map and Sim
  SPEC.md
```

## 5. WAD & asset handling

### 5.1 WAD layer
- Parse the IWAD and PWAD headers and lump directories. Later PWADs override lumps by name. Namespace markers (`S_START/S_END`, `F_START/F_END`, `P_START/P_END`, including the `SS_`/`FF_` variants) are respected.
- The IWAD is located through a configurable path, the game folder, or common install locations (Steam/GOG). If none is found, a file picker is shown, with the optional shareware download (T1.1b).

### 5.2 Decoders
| Data | Lumps | Output |
|---|---|---|
| Palette | `PLAYPAL` (14 palettes) | Palette texture. Palettes 1–13 drive the damage, pickup and radsuit screen tints |
| Light | `COLORMAP` (34 maps) | Colormap texture for the palette shader (see §7.4) |
| Wall textures | `TEXTURE1`/`TEXTURE2` + `PNAMES` + patches | Composite textures, stored as 8-bit palette indices |
| Flats | `F_START..F_END` | 64×64 index textures. Animated flats (`NUKAGE`, `FWATER`, …) come from the hardcoded animation table |
| Sprites | `S_START..S_END` | Frames with rotations 0–8 and mirrored pairs (e.g. `TROOA2A8`), plus offsets |
| UI graphics | `STBAR`, `STTNUM*`, `M_*`, `WI*`, `TITLEPIC`, `CREDIT`, `HELP*`, fonts `STCFN*` | Textures for the HUD, menus and intermission |
| Sounds | `DS*` (DMX format, 8-bit, usually 11025 Hz) | `AudioStreamWAV` |
| Music | `D_*` (MUS format) + `GENMIDI` (OPL instrument bank) | Played through OPL3 emulation (see §7.7) |
| Maps | `THINGS, LINEDEFS, SIDEDEFS, VERTEXES, SEGS, SSECTORS, NODES, SECTORS, REJECT, BLOCKMAP` | Map data structures |

All indexed graphics stay **8-bit indexed** on the GPU (R8 textures). The palette is applied in the shader. This allows authentic light diminishing through `COLORMAP`, palette flashes, and the invulnerability colormap.

### 5.3 Mod formats beyond vanilla (not supported yet)

v1 plays **vanilla-format PWADs** loaded over the IWAD with `-file` (§2, §5.1). That covers the map lumps, textures, flats, sprites, sounds, `D_*` music (MUS, and MIDI as Chocolate Doom's OPL player reads it) and DeePsea tall patches (T1.3a). Everything else in this section is out of scope for v1 (§1 *Non-goals*). It is listed here because players will try it: after the IWADs, community PWADs are what people play Doom with, and the large, popular ones (most of the Cacowards of the last decade) target the formats below, not vanilla.

**Survey of eight vanilla-era sets** (2026-10-14, then validated map by map in `docs/PWAD-VALIDATION.md` P1–P12, 2026-10-18/19; SPEC §12 "P1"–"P11"): Scythe, Alien Vendetta (AV, with `AVMOVFIX`), Memento Mori (MM), Doom 2 the Way id Did (D2TWID), Requiem and Hell Revealed (HR) over Doom II; Doom the Way id Did (DTWID) and No End in Sight (NEIS) over The Ultimate Doom. All are vanilla or limit-removing (AV, NEIS's E4).

- **What plays: all eight.** Every map of every set loads and passes the headless level check, MM's reversed and NUL-padded map names included. Every graphic, sound and song passes the viewer check, and the songs (MUS and MIDI) match Chocolate Doom's OPL player write for write. All 28 demo lumps match the vanilla reference on every tic. Each set's first map is walked to its exit against vanilla, through its own intermission into its second map, with the wipes and songs. Requiem's Doom II text screens and cast call match vanilla too. D2TWID's `PLAYPAL` and `COLORMAP` are used everywhere. Each set's UI graphics are drawn, and the wide bar's panels fall back to `FLAT5_4` beside a 320-wide `STBAR`. A netgame on Scythe and on DTWID plays in sync, and a join with other PWADs is refused. What vanilla ignores loads, does nothing, and is logged: NEIS's MBF sky transfers (271, 272) and its ZDoom special 307 (E4M6 is still finishable), AV MAP27's Boom scroller (85), AV MAP04's Boom thing flag, and the `DEHACKED`, `MAPINFO` and `ZMAPINFO` lumps. No exit reaches D2TWID's MAP33 or NEIS's E1M0 and E4M0, as in vanilla. MAP33 is reached by `--level`, `idclev33` and the warp and Host Game lists; ExM0 only by `--level`.
- **Where vanilla fails too** (limit-removing maps; each a decision for the user): `MAXPLATS` on Requiem MAP27 (line 685) and NEIS E3M7, where vanilla errors and the game now holds (T5.5a, T7.13). `spechit` overruns with every monster awake on Scythe MAP18 and MAP30, AV MAP15 and MAP21, and Requiem MAP31; vanilla leaves sync on Scythe MAP30 (T4.4a). NEIS E4M7's block lists past offset 32767, where vanilla crashes and the sim plays (T4.3a).
- **Our own gaps:** the 1 s load budget on AV MAP10, MAP11, MAP20 (2.5 s) and MAP25–MAP27, on NEIS E3M7, E4M2 and E4M5–E4M8 (2.4 s), and on D2TWID MAP31 (T2.9d). The 2 ms tic budget with every monster awake on NEIS E3M7 (p95 2.17 ms), plus one 5.2 ms tic on AV MAP20 (T8.10). The turned automap overflows fixed_t on NEIS E4M7 (T7.9d). A few drawn-pixel views fail with a renderer on the biggest maps: masked middles with a negative row offset on D2TWID MAP31 and AV MAP20, and views whose subject the map hides (T2.6b). Sides with a `-` texture are common, 150–2,661 per set (NEIS E4M8 alone has 1,123, built for ports without the hall of mirrors), as are BSP node artefacts (T2.3a, T2.2a). Grey panels sit beside a themed `STBAR` (T6.13z). RouteGen needs hints on AV, D2TWID and Requiem (T5.5b). The 2024 `doom.wad` lists E4M1 after E4M9 (T9.1a). The PWAD netgame and its refusal are not run in CI (T11.6b). An unused sidedef naming sector 65535 (MM, Requiem) was fixed, as were a sector light above 255 (D2TWID) and fog on doors whose special the sim clears (NEIS).
- **Still open:** a person's play of two or three maps per set on the desktop and the Steam Deck, and frame times on real hardware. Lavapipe's 20–46 ms a frame in the dev container says nothing about §9's 60 fps (P10, T8.11).

**What each post-v1 format would add for these eight:**

- **DeHackEd:** four sets ship a patch. AV's (40 texts), DTWID's (36) and NEIS's (40) change only strings: text screens and level names. D2TWID's changes 3 things (Keen → Dopefish and Yorp, Garg), 52 frames, 15 codepointers and 37 texts. So D2TWID plays differently without its patch, and its demos are checked only against an unpatched reference (P6).
- **UMAPINFO:** none of the eight ships one. D2TWID's `MAPINFO`/`ZMAPINFO` and NEIS's `MAPINFO` are ZDoom dialects, never read. They are what would lead exits to MAP33, E1M0 and E4M0.
- **Boom/MBF:** little. NEIS's sky transfers matter little, since the sky is never drawn (§7.2). AV MAP27 would gain its scroller, and AV MAP04 would keep its 24 not-in-co-op things out of co-op. A Boom complevel would lift the limits above (T5.5a, T4.4a, T4.3a). No set needs extended nodes or another map format.

| Format | What it adds | Who uses it | Player interest | Fit with IsoDoom | Verdict |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| **DeHackEd** (`.deh`, `-deh`, a `DEHACKED` lump) | Patches the executable's tables: `mobjinfo`, `states` (frames and codepointers), weapons, ammo, sounds, par times, cheats and text strings | Most vanilla-era PWADs with new monsters, weapons or story text. D2TWID's patch changes 3 things, 52 frames, 15 codepointers and 37 texts; Alien Vendetta's, DTWID's and NEIS's change only text | **High.** Without it some PWADs play wrong, not just look wrong | **Good.** It edits the same tables the sim ports, with no new behaviour. The sim's tables (`Info.states`, `Info.mobjinfo`) are `static readonly` now and would become per-world copies, patched at load and part of the checksum and the saves. The strings (d_englsh.h) would move into a patchable table | **First after v1.** Small, vanilla-accurate (Chocolate Doom's `deh_*.c` is the reference), and a prerequisite for BEX and MBF21 |
| **UMAPINFO** lump | Episode menu entries, level names, the next and secret maps, par times, music, intermission and ending screens, per map | Many modern PWADs, including some vanilla-format ones, to get their names and episodes right in ports | **Medium.** Without it the menu and intermission show the IWAD's names | **Good.** It touches only the game shell (M7's menus, intermission and finale), not the sim | **Second.** Cheap; the ZDoom `MAPINFO` dialects stay unsupported |
| **Boom** (complevel 9; PrBoom+, DSDA-Doom) | Generalized line and sector specials, scrollers, conveyors, wind, friction, pushers, `TRANMAP` translucency, `ANIMATED`/`SWITCHES` lumps, coloured lighting (`COLORMAP` lumps), deep water (`242`), sky transfers (`271`/`272`), silent teleports, and BEX (DeHackEd with names and extra codepointers) | Most community megawads since the 2000s: Sunlust, Ancient Aliens and many Cacoward winners | **Very high.** It's the de facto community standard | **Large but possible.** The sim ports p_spec.c's Boom changes from PrBoom+/DSDA-Doom (GPL-2.0+), behind a complevel switch so vanilla stays exact (§6.3). Translucency and coloured light fit the palette shader (lookups like `COLORMAP`). Deep water (`242`) is the hard part: its fake floors and ceilings must become real geometry under the iso camera and the cutaway. It also needs extended nodes (below) | **Third.** Needs its own milestone and its own vanilla-reference harness (DSDA-Doom demos) |
| **Extended nodes** (ZDBSP's `XNOD`/`ZNOD`/`XGLN`, DeePBSP) | Node formats with no 32k limit, required by large maps | Large Boom and MBF21 maps; some limit-removing maps | Only as part of Boom | **Medium.** The sim walks the BSP (`R_PointInSubsector`, sight checks); the mesh does not need it. T2.1a refuses these formats clearly until then | With Boom |
| **MBF / MBF21** | MBF: friends (the helper dog), bouncing, extra codepointers. MBF21: new thing and frame flags, parameterised codepointers, all in DeHackEd | Many 2020s releases (Eviternity at MBF, Eviternity II at MBF21) | **High** among current players | **Good on top of Boom and DeHackEd**: still a vanilla-style tic sim with tables, deterministic | **Fourth**, after Boom |
| **ID24** (the 2024 Doom + Doom II re-release's spec; DSDHacked) | Unlimited DeHackEd indices, new codepointers, `SBARDEF`, `GAMECONF`, and more. Legacy of Rust's `id1.wad` needs it | The re-release's add-ons; new releases | **Rising.** Owners of the 2024 re-release have these files | Builds on MBF21 | **Watch.** Revisit after MBF21 |
| **Hexen format, UDMF, ZDoom/GZDoom** (ACS, DECORATE, ZScript, slopes, 3D floors, PK3) | A general scripting engine | GZDoom gameplay mods (Brutal Doom and similar) and ZDoom-only maps | High, but these players already have GZDoom | **Poor.** It's a different engine: arbitrary scripts and true 3D geometry break the deterministic vanilla sim (§6.1), and the camera and cutaway assume Doom's 2.5D sectors | **Never.** Refuse clearly with a message (T2.1a) |

**Order after v1:** DeHackEd, then UMAPINFO, then Boom (with extended nodes and BEX), then MBF21. Each sits behind a compatibility level chosen from the WAD (a `COMPLVL` lump or the presence of its lumps) or `-complevel`, so the vanilla sim and its route and demo tests stay exact (§6.3, §10). A PWAD the game cannot load (an unknown node format, a Hexen map) is refused with a message saying what it needs, not loaded wrong. A vanilla map with a few later-port specials loads as vanilla loads it: the specials do nothing, and the game logs them (SPEC §12 "P7"). A Boom sector special the player stands in is vanilla's error (T7.13).

## 6. Gameplay

### 6.1 Simulation core
- A fixed **35 Hz** tic loop. Rendering runs at the display refresh rate and interpolates actor positions between tics.
- **Fixed-point 16.16** arithmetic, the original `finesine`/`finetangent` tables, and the original `rndtable` (`P_Random`/`M_Random`). This gives faithful behaviour and determinism.
- Port of the relevant parts of the original source:
  - `info.c` (state, mobjinfo and sprite tables). This is the data-driven heart of monsters, weapons and items.
  - `p_mobj`, `p_map`, `p_maputl` (movement, collision, blockmap, line-of-sight via `REJECT` and `P_CheckSight`).
  - `p_enemy` (monster AI and action functions), `p_pspr` (weapon states and firing), `p_inter` (damage and pickups).
  - `p_spec`, `p_doors`, `p_plats`, `p_floor`, `p_ceilng`, `p_lights`, `p_switch`, `p_telept` (map specials).
  - `p_user` (player movement, adapted for twin-stick; see §6.3).
- **Determinism rules** (these keep lockstep co-op possible later):
  - No `float`/`double` anywhere in `Sim/`. All positions, angles, speeds and momentum use fixed point or BAM angles, as in vanilla.
  - The only input to the sim is one `ticcmd` per player per tic. The absolute aim angle is quantised to a 16-bit angle before it enters the `ticcmd`.
  - No wall-clock time, no `System.Random`, and no iteration over unordered collections (`Dictionary`/`HashSet` ordering) in the sim. Thinkers are kept in a linked list, as in vanilla.
  - The sim supports N players from the start (a `players[]` array, as in vanilla), even though v1 runs only one.
  - A cheap **state checksum** is computed per tic (positions, health, `P_Random` index). It is used in tests now and for desync detection in co-op later.
  - An analyser or test enforces the no-float rule (for example, a reflection/IL scan of the `Sim` assembly).
- Skill levels 1–5, including Nightmare (fast monsters and respawn) and the `-nomonsters`-style options exposed in a menu.

### 6.2 Controls (twin-stick)
| Action | Default (KB+M) | Gamepad |
|---|---|---|
| Move | WASD (relative to the screen, not to facing) | Left stick |
| Aim | Mouse cursor on the ground plane | Right stick |
| Fire | LMB | RT |
| Use (open/switch) | E / Space | A |
| Weapon select | 1–7, mouse wheel | LB/RB |
| Run toggle | Shift | Left-stick click |
| Automap | Tab | Back |
| Menu | Esc | Start |

- The player's body angle follows the aim direction. Movement is independent of facing (strafing in all directions at full speed, using vanilla forward and side speeds normalised so diagonals are not faster than intended).
- Input is converted into a vanilla-style `ticcmd` (forward/side move plus absolute angle) before it reaches the sim.

### 6.3 Necessary deviations from vanilla ("top-down tweaks")
Each tweak is a named option in the sim, so the pure vanilla behaviour stays testable.

1. **Absolute aiming.** The player angle is set directly from the cursor each tic instead of being turned incrementally.
2. **Horizontal aim assist.** Hitscan and projectiles snap to the nearest valid target within a small cone (for example ±5°) around the cursor direction. Vanilla vertical autoaim (`P_AimLineAttack`) is kept, because the player cannot aim vertically.
3. **Use action.** The use trace starts from the player in the aim direction. If it hits nothing, the nearest usable line within `USERANGE` facing the player is used instead, so doors do not need pixel-precise aiming.
4. **No first-person weapon sprites.** The weapon state machine (`psprites`) still runs for timing, sounds and muzzle flash. The muzzle flash is shown as a light or sprite effect on the player in the world. The current weapon appears in the HUD.
5. **Visibility of enemies.** Monster AI still uses vanilla sight checks. The player sees everything not hidden by the cutaway, which matches Isowulf's "you see them before they see you".
6. **Spectre / partial invisibility.** Rendered with a shader-based fuzz effect that approximates the vanilla fuzz.

## 7. Presentation

### 7.1 Camera
- A **fixed isometric-style camera**, the same as Isowulf. It does not rotate. It is an orthographic camera (decided in T3.3, §12; a narrow-FOV perspective camera stays a presentation option) looking down at roughly 45–60° pitch and 45° yaw, centred on the player with slight smoothing and look-ahead towards the cursor.
- Zoom is adjustable within limits using the mouse wheel with a modifier, or through options.
- Scale: 1 Doom map unit = 1/32 m in Godot (a player is 56 units ≈ 1.75 m).

### 7.2 Level geometry
Built procedurally from map data at level load:

- **Walls:** for each linedef and sidedef:
  - One-sided: middle texture from floor to ceiling.
  - Two-sided: upper texture (where the ceiling difference is), lower texture (where the floor difference is), and an optional masked middle texture (grates, fences) with alpha cut (one on one side only is drawn from behind too, mirrored: §12 T3.1a). An upper wall over a sector with a lower ceiling is drawn only where that sector's ceiling moves (doors, crushers) or its lid shows (lintels, windows, closed doors): over a room or a step down to a lower ceiling, which is never drawn, it would hang in the air as a band (§12, *Upper walls*; an option, on by default).
  - Texture alignment follows vanilla rules: x/y offsets and the lower/upper unpegged flags (`ML_DONTPEGTOP`, `ML_DONTPEGBOTTOM`).
  - Each side is drawn per seg, along the floor's corners and with the seg offsets, as vanilla draws segs, so walls meet floors without cracks (§12, T2.9).
  - **Wall caps** (T6.13e, §12): each one-sided wall gets a top, the strip of the void 16 units deep behind it at its top (its sector's ceiling, or its lid), showing the wall's texture, while the wall faces the camera, so walls read as blocks rather than sheets (an option, on by default). The walls of a small **island** (a pillar: a void enclosed by one loop of walls, at most 128 units across) all reach its highest wall's top, drawn on above their ceilings, so it reads as one column. The rest of the void stays black.
  - **Switch markers** (T6.13p, §12): the fixed camera never sees a wall facing away from it (north or west), so a switch on one gets a marker on the floor in front of it: the switch's rows of its current texture laid back onto the floor, as deep as the switch (16–32 units), mirrored so they read from behind the wall, shown while the wall faces away (an option, on by default).
- **Height cues** (T6.13s, §12): seen from above a raised floor reads as one more floor, and the wall below a ledge's edge is culled whenever it faces away from the camera. Each **ledge** (a floor more than 24 units, the player's step, above the floor across a two-sided line) gets a bright line, 3 units wide with a dark inner rim, along its top edge, following lifts and moving floors; as another style, the floors are **shaded** by their height, a light level brighter per 24 units above the player's feet and darker below, at most 5, the darkening stopping at light level 8 of 16 so a deep drop never goes black (an option: edges by default, shade, both or off).
- **Floors:** the sector floor polygons are built by **clipping each subsector against its BSP partition lines** (the classic approach source ports use for vanilla nodes). The resulting convex polygons are triangulated (a fan, with neighbours' corners on their edges inserted first so no T-junctions crack the floor; §12, T2.4), and the triangles are grouped per sector. This is robust against the unclosed or malformed sectors found in vanilla maps.
- **Ceilings:** never rendered (see §7.3). Ceiling heights are still used for wall tops, the sim and crushers. The exception is the **lids** (T6.13b, §12): the solid above a thin sector whose ceiling is below all its neighbours' (closed doors, the lintels over open ones, windows) gets a top at the lowest neighbouring ceiling, showing the sector's ceiling flat, so a closed door reads as a block rather than two faces with a slot between them (an option, on by default). The block's **ends** (T6.13f, §12) close it where a jamb faces away from the camera (and so has no cap): each lid sector's one-sided wall again, from its ceiling up to its lid, facing the void.
- **Moving sectors:** each sector's floor and its adjoining wall quads are kept in per-sector mesh chunks. Floor and ceiling heights are passed as per-sector values (a data texture or uniform buffer indexed by sector id). A vertex shader positions those vertices, so doors, lifts and crushers move without rebuilding the mesh.
- **Sky:** with no ceilings there is no sky to draw. Out-of-map void is black or a dark backdrop. Walls whose upper texture is sky (`F_SKY1` on both sides) are not drawn, as in vanilla.
- **Animated textures and flats, and scrolling walls** (linedef special 48) are handled in the shader through per-surface parameters.

### 7.3 Occlusion / cutaway
- No ceilings are drawn.
- Walls between the camera and the player (and, optionally, the cursor) are **cut down or faded**. In the level shader, fragments within a radius of the line from the player towards the camera (a disc on screen) whose plane the player is behind, from the camera's view, are discarded above a cutoff height (32 units above the player's floor) or dithered. Floors that high (the tops of blocks whose walls are cut) are cut the same way when they lie on the camera's side of the player (a floor or lid behind the player cannot hide it and stays whole); the player's own floor, and every floor up to the cutoff, never is (§12, T3.4, T6.13c).
- Walls far from the player stay at full height so the level keeps its shape.
- Tuned in T3.4 (§12): radius 80 units, cutoff 32, cut (dither and off are options), the player only (the cursor ground point is an option).
- A raised floor cut this way is **capped** at the cutoff (T3.4a, §12): inside the disc its flat is drawn again at the cutoff height, darker, so the block reads as cut down to the stub its walls keep rather than hollow (an option: dark, flat or off). Solid areas outside every sector (one-sided walls' backs) still show the void, but for the wall caps (T6.13e), which are cut as floors are, but only where their wall is (the centre behind the wall's plane: a player standing in front of a wall keeps its top whole, as its face), and capped at the cutoff as well, so a cut wall reads as a block cut down to its stub.
- **Lids** (T6.13b, §12) are cut as floors are (their ends, T6.13f, as walls), and the cap also caps the solid under a lid where it spans the cutoff (a closed door), so a cut door reads as a 32-unit block with a top. A door the player stands in front of (on the camera's side) stays whole, lid included (T6.13c). **Doors are never cut by default** (T6.13d, §12): nothing of a sector a door special moves (its walls, lid and floor) is cut, from either side; cutting them as the rest is an option (`--level-cutaway-doors=cut`).
- **Things** in front of the player are cut by the same rule (T3.4b, §12): a billboard whose foot is in front of the player's anchor loses what lies above the cutoff inside the disc, as a wall through its foot facing the camera would. By default only decorations and pickups; actors (`MF_SHOOTABLE`: monsters, barrels, the player; and missiles, `MF_MISSILE` types) stay whole, since they must stay visible to be shot or dodged (an option: decor, all or off).
- **Fog of war** (T6.13l, §12): under the game camera, the sectors the player has not seen are **not drawn** by default (dimmed to dark grey as an option, or everything drawn with it off), sectors it has seen keep their geometry, and monsters, missiles and barrels are drawn in every sector it has seen, live, as items are (T6.13n; with `--level-fog-things=sight`, only while their sector is in its sight). Line of sight is a 360° walk of the BSP from the player after each tic, as vanilla's renderer clips, which also sets vanilla's `ML_MAPPED` for the automap (§7.6).

### 7.4 Lighting
- **Authentic mode (default):** a palette shader. The colour index is looked up in `COLORMAP` through vanilla's light tables (r_main.c `zlight`/`scalelight`, r_segs.c's fake contrast), using the sector light level and, for light diminishing, the horizontal distance from the player, at least 80 units (view-distance diminishing makes little sense top-down; T3.7); no diminishing and camera depth are presentation options (§12, T2.8).
- Sector light specials (flicker, strobe, glow, blink) come from `p_lights` and are updated per tic.
- Fullbright sprite frames (`FF_FULLBRIGHT`) ignore the sector light.
- Palette flashes: red when damaged, gold on pickups, green with the radsuit (`PLAYPAL` 1–13), applied by switching the palette the shaders look up (vanilla's `I_SetPalette`; §12 T6.8). Invulnerability and the light amplification visor force a colormap (32, 1) everywhere. Options (§12 T6.13v): a strength (10–100 %, the flash palette blended from palette 0; 100, the default, is vanilla's palettes exactly), and where they show: the whole screen (vanilla), on and around the player only (the default since T6.13w: its own sprite in the flash palette and a glow on the floor at its feet, a pickup's white rather than gold and, for the glow, a white line circle growing to the aim marker's ring, the view on palette 0), or off (no flashes and no power-up colormaps).
- *Optional* enhanced mode: real Godot lights for muzzle flashes, fireballs and plasma.

### 7.5 Sprites (monsters, items, projectiles, player)
- Rendered as Y-axis billboards (upright, facing the camera), sized from the patch dimensions and offsets at 1:1 map scale, and anchored at the thing's height (its floor, or for `MF_SPAWNCEILING` things the ceiling minus their height). The rows below a patch's origin draw over the floor, as in vanilla; sprites sort against walls, masked middles and each other by depth (§12, T3.5). **Readability (§12, T3.6):** the billboards tilt towards the camera (by default fully, so a patch shows at its drawn proportions instead of foreshortened like the walls) while keeping an upright billboard's depth, and get a one-texel black outline; each is a presentation option. **Against walls (§12, T3.5a):** a billboard is pulled towards the camera along each view ray by its thing's radius (at most 16 units), so a wall the thing stands against behind it does not cut it, as vanilla draws such a sprite whole. **Behind walls (§12, T3.6a):** a fully tilted billboard is 1.74× as tall on screen as an upright one, so a thing behind a wall can show its head above it where its upright billboard stays hidden; by default it does (as in most iso games), and an option drops a billboard whole when the level would hide its upright billboard whole. **In the dark (§12, T4.7a):** the player's own sprite is lit as if its sector's light were at least 128 (an option), so it stays visible in dark rooms, which stay dark.
- **Item beams** (T6.13r, §12): a pickup the level hides whole under the game camera (a wall hides the floor up to about 0.7 × its height behind it) gets a thin vertical beam rising from it, coloured by its kind, depth-tested so it comes up from behind the wall; only while the item is hidden, only for items the fog of war shows (an option: all pickups by default, or keys, weapons and power-ups only, or off).
- **Sight dim** (T6.13t, §12): a live monster with no line of sight to the player (vanilla's `P_CheckSight`, monster to player: the test that lets it attack) is drawn darker under the game camera, its colormap row pushed down 6 rows by default (at most to row 29), so the monsters that can hit the player stand out from those the camera sees over a wall, ledge or pillar. It fades out of sight after a short grace and comes back at once (an option: the rows, or off).
- **Monster markers** (T6.13u, §12): a live monster with a line of sight to the player (the sight dim's `P_CheckSight`) that the level hides whole under the game camera gets a marker: a small red chevron, pointing down, on top of what hides it, or a red beam as the item beams', rising from behind it; only while it is hidden, never for one out of sight (an option: beam (the default), chevron or off).
- **Rotation selection:** for 8-rotation sprites, the frame is chosen from the angle between the actor's facing and the fixed camera's view direction, using the same formula as vanilla `R_ProjectSprite` with the camera as the viewpoint. Mirrored rotations flip the UV.
- The player is drawn with the `PLAY*` sprites. All weapons share the same body frames, as in vanilla multiplayer.
- Sector light applies through the palette shader (vanilla's `scalelight` without the fake contrast, at the distance walls use; full-bright frames colormap 0; a fixed colormap overrides both). Partial invisibility uses the fuzz effect (§12 T6.9: vanilla's `fuzzoffset` rows through colormap 6, read from the screen in a second pass).
- Corpses, gibs and decorations remain as vanilla things.
- A blob shadow under actors is optional (for depth readability): implemented in T3.6 (blended or dithered, under things with `MF_SHOOTABLE` and the player), off by default (§12).

### 7.6 HUD & UI
- **HUD:** the original `STBAR` status bar (ammo, health, arms, face, armor, keys, ammo table), scaled at the bottom of the screen. A minimal "fullscreen" HUD is optional. The ready weapon shows as an icon (its pickup sprite, at half the HUD's scale) just above the bar's ammo counter, or above the ammo in the fullscreen HUD, and its digit in the bar's ARMS panel brighter, since no first-person weapon is drawn (§6.3 #4); two options. *T6.13x (§12).* By default the bar is 400 wide, filling a wide window at a smaller scale and so shorter: vanilla's 320 columns in its middle as they are, a panel each side, the left one holding the weapon icon beside the ammo; an option gives vanilla's 320 back, with the icon above the bar. *T6.13y (§12).*
- **Aspect:** vanilla's 320×200 screen (the HUD, the menus, the title loop, the intermission, the finale, the automap's frame) shows by default at 4:3, its pixels 1.2 times taller than wide as on the CRTs it was drawn for; square pixels are an option. *T7.11 (§12).*
- Messages ("Picked up a shotgun.") use the original font (`STCFN*`).
- **Pointer:** on a level under the game camera the mouse pointer is a crosshair, the system's arrow elsewhere; an option. *T7.12 (§12).*
- **Automap:** an overlay version of the vanilla automap, drawn from line data with the usual colours and secret and seen-lines rules. *T7.9 (§12):* am_map.c ported as vanilla, its seen lines the fog of war's; as wide as the window at the HUD's scale under the status bar, black or (option) over the level, at vanilla's pixels or (option) the window's; Tab or the pad's Y. *T7.9c (§12):* turned by default to the game camera's view (its up the screen's), north up as an option.
- **Menus:** recreated in Godot with the original graphics (`M_DOOM`, `M_NGAME`, `M_SKULL1/2`, etc.): New Game → Episode → Skill, Options, Load/Save, Quit with its quit messages.
- **Intermission screens** (`WI*` graphics: kills, items, secrets, time, par) and the episode **finale text screens** and end sequences (`VICTORY2`, `ENDPIC`, the E3 bunny scroller, the Doom II cast call later).
- The title, demo loop and credits show `TITLEPIC`/`CREDIT`/`HELP`. Demo playback is **not** included.

### 7.7 Audio
- Sound effects use positional 2D/3D audio from the actor's position relative to the player, with vanilla's sound limits and priorities approximated. *T7.7 (§12):* s_sound.c's channels, priorities, attenuation and separation as vanilla's, the separation measured from the screen's up direction. Per-sector sound propagation for monster alerting stays in the sim (`P_NoiseAlert`).
- **Music (authentic OPL, default):** the `D_*` MUS lumps are played directly through an emulated **OPL3 (AdLib/Sound Blaster FM)** chip, using the instrument bank in the WAD's `GENMIDI` lump. This reproduces the sound of the original DOS release and uses only original assets.
  - Emulator: a C# port of **Nuked-OPL3** (LGPL-2.1, GPL-compatible). If it costs too much CPU, a lighter emulator (DOSBox DBOPL) is the fallback.
  - Driver: a port of Chocolate Doom's `i_oplmusic.c` (GPL-2.0), which handles the MUS sequencer, voice allocation and OPL2/OPL3 modes.
  - Output: a C# `AudioStreamGenerator` fed from a dedicated thread at 44.1/48 kHz with a small ring buffer. *T7.8e (§12):* the generator's own ring buffer, at least two output-driver periods; the volume is the driver's (the notes' levels), the bus only mutes at 0. *T7.8g (§12):* OPL3 (the default) or OPL2, an option (More Options → Sound, `--level-opl`), switched at once: the song again from its start on a new driver, the old one faded out under it.
- **Music (optional, later):** a General MIDI mode through a software synth such as MeltySynth, with a user-supplied SF2 soundfont, for players who prefer the SC-55/GUS sound.

## 8. Game flow & persistence
- Episode/map progression, secret exits (E1M3 → E1M9, etc.), and end-of-episode finales.
- Par times from the vanilla tables.
- **Save/load:** a serialised sim state in a custom format (vanilla-compatible save files are not a goal). Quicksave and quickload are included.
- **Options:** controls/rebinding, mouse sensitivity, audio volumes, video (resolution, fullscreen, vsync, zoom), gameplay tweaks (aim-assist strength, cutaway style, authentic vs enhanced lighting).
- Cheats: `iddqd`, `idkfa`, `idclip`, `idclev`, `iddt` (automap). These are cheap to port and useful for testing. *T7.9a (§12):* all of st_stuff.c's but `iddt` (T7.9), typed on the keyboard, carried to the sim in the `ticcmd`. *T7.9 (§12):* `iddt` is the automap's own (presentation only, not in the `ticcmd`). *T7.9b (§12):* a pad gets them through a cheats page in the options (More Options → Cheats).

## 9. Performance targets
- 60+ fps at 1080p on integrated graphics for all Doom 1 maps.
- **Steam Deck:** a locked 60 fps at 1280×800 (native Linux build) on all Doom 1 and Doom II maps, with headroom for battery life (a 40/30 fps cap option).
- Level load (WAD parse, mesh build) under 1 s per map.
- The sim stays under 2 ms per tic with a full map (e.g. E1M9 or MAP29-class monster counts).

## 10. Testing
- **Unit tests** for the WAD reader, lump namespaces, decoders (palette, patch, flat composition) and map parsing, run against `DOOM1.WAD` when it is present.
- **Sim tests** with tweaks disabled: fixed-point maths and table checks, `P_Random` sequence, line-special behaviour on small synthetic maps.
- *Stretch:* verify the sim core against vanilla demos (`DEMO1–3` in `DOOM1.WAD`) with all tweaks off and a vanilla-input adapter. A demo that stays in sync is strong proof that the port is faithful. *Holds since T6.13 (§12): `DEMO1`–`DEMO3` stay in sync to their end (`DemoSyncTests`).*
- Visual checks with a debug overlay showing sector ids, linedef ids and blockmap cells.

## 11. Milestones

| # | Milestone | Done when |
|---|---|---|
| M0 | Project skeleton | Godot 4 C# project, CI build, tests run |
| M1 | WAD viewer | Loads `DOOM1.WAD`; lists lumps; shows palette, textures, flats and sprites in a debug scene |
| M2 | Static level render | E1M1 is rendered as a 3D mesh with correct textures, alignment, light levels and the palette shader; free-fly debug camera |
| M3 | Iso camera + cutaway | Fixed iso camera following a placeholder; walls cut away; masked midtextures; things rendered as billboards with rotations |
| M4 | Sim core: movement | 35 Hz tic loop; twin-stick player with vanilla movement and collision (blockmap, step-up height, dropoffs); interpolation |
| M5 | Map specials | Doors, lifts, switches, teleporters, light specials, damage floors, exits; keys; E1M1–E1M9 completable without monsters |
| M6 | Monsters & weapons | `info.c` states, AI, all Doom 1 shareware weapons and monsters, pickups, damage, aim assist; status bar HUD |
| M7 | Game shell | Menus, skill and episode select, intermission, finale, save/load, options, sound effects, OPL music, shareware download (if cheap; see §3) |
| M8 | Shareware complete | All of E1 playable start to finish on desktop Linux and the Steam Deck; polish pass |
| M9 | Ultimate Doom | E2–E4, bosses, registered-only content |
| M10 | Doom II | Doom II monsters and weapons, MAPxx flow, cast call, Icon of Sin; then Final Doom |
| M11 | Multiplayer | Online co-op and deathmatch for up to 4 players in lockstep over ENet, hosted and joined from the menus; multi-player games match vanilla's netdemos tic by tic (`docs/plans/multiplayer.md`) |

| Date | Decision |
|---|---|
| 2026-10-05 | Godot 4 + C#, desktop only. Linux (Steam Deck) is a first-class target |
| 2026-10-05 | Fixed isometric camera (no rotation), no ceilings, wall cutaway |
| 2026-10-05 | Faithful port of the vanilla sim, with switchable top-down tweaks |
| 2026-10-05 | Music via OPL3 emulation + `GENMIDI` (authentic); a GM soundfont mode is optional later |
| 2026-10-05 | Shareware auto-download if cheap to implement, otherwise deferred |
| 2026-10-05 | The sim is deterministic and float-free to keep co-op possible; revisit if it gets in the way |
| 2026-10-05 | Target .NET 10 (LTS, supported to Nov 2028) instead of .NET 8 (support ends Nov 2026). Godot's C# libraries target .NET 8 but run fine in a `net10.0` project; Godot-generated `.csproj` files default to `net8.0` and must be changed |
| 2026-10-05 | **Pinned toolchain (T0.1):** Godot **4.7.2-stable .NET** (`Godot_v4.7.2-stable_mono_linux_x86_64`, the latest stable at the time), `Godot.NET.Sdk/4.7.2`, .NET SDK **10.0.401** (runtime 10.0.12), `net10.0` everywhere. Install on Linux: the dev container (`.devcontainer/Dockerfile`) builds on `mcr.microsoft.com/devcontainers/dotnet:2-10.0-noble` and unpacks the Godot .NET zip from the GitHub release into `/opt/godot`, with a `godot` wrapper in `/usr/local/bin`. Without the container: install the .NET 10 SDK (Microsoft's packages or `dotnet-install.sh`) and unzip the same Godot .NET build anywhere on `PATH`. Bump versions by changing `GODOT_VERSION` in `devcontainer.json`/`Dockerfile` and the SDK version in `IsoDoom.csproj` together |
| 2026-10-05 | **Project split:** the repo root is the Godot project (`project.godot`, `IsoDoom.csproj`, `IsoDoom.sln`). `src/Wad`, `src/Map` and `src/Sim` are plain `Microsoft.NET.Sdk` class libraries (`IsoDoom.Wad`, `IsoDoom.Map`, `IsoDoom.Sim`) with no Godot reference, so a Godot type in them is a compile error. References: `Map → Wad`, `Sim → Map`, and the Godot project references all three. `Assets`, `Render`, `Game` and `Audio` stay in the Godot project. `IsoDoom.csproj` excludes `src/{Wad,Map,Sim}/**` and `tests/**` from its default glob. Shared library settings (nullable, warnings as errors, no implicit usings) live in `src/Directory.Build.props` |
| 2026-10-05 | `IsoDoom.sln` keeps Godot's solution configurations (`Debug`, `ExportDebug`, `ExportRelease`); the plain libraries map them to `Debug`/`Debug`/`Release`, so Godot export builds work |
| 2026-10-05 | `.gdignore` files keep Godot from scanning `src/Wad`, `src/Map`, `src/Sim`, `tests`, `wads` and `doom_20230531` (no `.import`/`.uid` files; the libraries hold no Godot scripts). Only the `.gdignore` markers in `wads/` and `doom_20230531/` are tracked |
| 2026-10-05 | **Tests (T0.2):** one xUnit project, `tests/IsoDoom.Tests` (`net10.0`, references Wad, Map and Sim, never Godot). Packages: `xunit.v3` 4.0.1 (Apache-2.0) and `Mono.Cecil` 0.11.6 (MIT); their transitive packages are MIT/Apache-2.0. They are test-only and never ship with the game. xunit.v3 4.x runs on Microsoft.Testing.Platform, and the .NET 10 SDK no longer bridges it to VSTest, so a root `global.json` sets `"test": {"runner": "Microsoft.Testing.Platform"}` (no `Microsoft.NET.Test.Sdk`/`xunit.runner.visualstudio`). Run `dotnet test` from the repo root. In `IsoDoom.sln` the test project builds only in `Debug`; `ExportDebug`/`ExportRelease` map it to `Debug`/`Release` without building it, so Godot exports don't build or restore test packages |
| 2026-10-05 | **Test WADs:** tests get the WAD path from `TestWads.RequireDoom1()`, which finds the repo root (the directory holding `IsoDoom.sln`) and uses `wads/DOOM1.WAD`, or the `ISODOOM_DOOM1_WAD` environment variable when it is set. The test is reported as *skipped* (xUnit v3 `Assert.Skip`) when the file is missing or its MD5 is not the shareware v1.9 one, since tests assert v1.9 contents |
| 2026-10-05 | **Sim purity scan:** `DeterminismScanner` (tests) reads the compiled `IsoDoom.Sim.dll` with Mono.Cecil and checks every type, compiler-generated ones included: base types, interfaces, generic constraints, field/property/event/parameter/return/local types and every IL operand (method, field, type and call-site references, unwrapped through generics, arrays, pointers, by-refs and modifiers). It flags `float`/`double`/`Half`/`MathF`/`System.Numerics` vector types and float opcodes (`ldc.r4/r8`, `conv.r4/r8`, `conv.r.un`, `ckfinite`); `System.Random`, `RandomNumberGenerator`, `Guid.NewGuid`, `HashCode` (randomly seeded per process); wall-clock time (`DateTime`, `DateTimeOffset`, `TimeProvider`, `Stopwatch`, timers, `Environment.TickCount`); and `GetEnumerator` on unordered collections (`Dictionary` and its key/value collections, `HashSet`, concurrent, frozen and immutable variants, `Hashtable`). Lookups in a `Dictionary` stay allowed. It cannot see an unordered collection enumerated through a plain `IEnumerable` (e.g. LINQ). Fixture classes in the test project prove each rule fires |
| 2026-10-05 | **CI (T0.3):** `.github/workflows/ci.yml` on GitHub Actions (`ubuntu-24.04`, actions pinned to major tags: `checkout@v5`, `setup-dotnet@v5`, `cache@v5`, `upload-artifact@v5`). Job `build-test`: restore, build and test `IsoDoom.sln` in Debug (WAD tests skip). Job `export-linux` (after `build-test`): downloads the pinned Godot .NET editor and export templates from the GitHub release, checks them against the release's `SHA512-SUMS.txt`, keeps only `linux_{release,debug}.x86_64` + `version.txt` of the templates, and caches editor + templates keyed on the Godot version. Then `godot --headless --import`, `--export-release "Linux"`, a 60-frame headless smoke run of the exported binary (must print `IsoDoom started`), and uploads `export/linux/` as artifact `IsoDoom-linux-x86_64` (14 days). `global.json` now also pins the SDK (`10.0.401`, `rollForward: latestFeature`) so `setup-dotnet` installs the same SDK as the dev container. A version bump now also touches `GODOT_VERSION` in `ci.yml` and the SDK in `global.json` |
| 2026-10-05 | **Export preset:** `export_presets.cfg` is committed (no secrets: no encryption key, SSH deploy off). One preset, `Linux` (x86_64, `all_resources`, PCK next to the binary, output `export/linux/IsoDoom.x86_64`, `export/` is gitignored). `exclude_filter` drops `wads/*`, `doom_20230531/*`, `tests/*`, `TestResults/*`, `export/*` and `global.json` as a backstop to the `.gdignore` markers; WAD data never ships in an export. The export directory must exist before exporting |
| 2026-10-05 | **WAD reader (T1.1):** `IsoDoom.Wad` has `WadFile` (one IWAD/PWAD; the whole file is read into a `byte[]`, lumps are `ReadOnlyMemory<byte>` views, so no open file handles and synthetic WADs load via `WadFile.FromBytes`) and `WadArchive` (the merged directory). Lump names are cut at the first NUL and upper-cased; lookups are case-insensitive and compare at most 8 characters. Bad magic or a directory/lump past the end of the file throws `WadFormatException`; zero-size lumps may have any position. Vanilla lookups keep vanilla names (`W_CheckNumForName`, `W_GetNumForName`, `W_LumpLength`, `W_CacheLumpNum`, `W_CacheLumpName`; w_wad.c): the last lump of a name in load order wins. The first file is not required to be an `IWAD` (vanilla doesn't check either); T1.2 decides what to accept |
| 2026-10-05 | **Namespaces (T1.1):** markers follow Chocolate Doom's w_merge.c: `S_`/`SS_`, `F_`/`FF_`, `P_`/`PP_` `START`/`END` open and close a namespace; `F1`–`F3` and `P1`–`P3` inner markers are markers inside it. Namespaces are tracked per file: an unclosed one ends with its file, and a stray end marker (or an inner marker outside its namespace) is a plain global lump. `WadArchive.GetNamespace` gives each namespace's merged contents without markers: a later lump with the same name replaces the earlier one *in place* (so flat animation ranges stay intact), new names are appended. This applies within a file too, so DOOM1's duplicate `SW18_7` patch collapses to the later copy (vanilla's `W_CheckNumForName` returns that one anyway). The namespace lists replace vanilla's `firstflat`/`firstspritelump` index ranges. Sprite merging is by whole lump name for now (frame-level replacement is a T1.5 note) |
| 2026-10-05 | **IWAD identification (T1.2):** `IwadIdentification.D_IdentifyVersion` ports Chocolate Doom's lump-based `D_IdentifyVersion` (d_main.c), since linuxdoom's `IdentifyVersion` only checks which IWAD file names exist. The first of `MAP01`/`E1M1` in the directory picks the mission (none: `WadFormatException`, "Unknown or invalid IWAD file"); for Doom 1, `E4M1` → `retail`, `E3M1` → `registered`, else `shareware`; `MAP01` → `commercial`. Chocolate Doom tells TNT and Plutonia from Doom II by file name; we use lumps unique to each IWAD instead (`REDTNT2` → `pack_tnt`, `CAMO1` → `pack_plut`, the same lumps GZDoom's iwadinfo checks), so renamed files still work. Only the first file's lumps count, so a PWAD cannot change the game mode, and **the first file must be an `IWAD`** (a `PWAD` there throws `WadFormatException`); this settles the T1.1 open point. The enums `GameMode` and `GameMission` (doomdef.h `GameMode_t`/`GameMission_t`) live in `IsoDoom.Wad` and keep vanilla's lower-case member names (`GameMode.commercial`, `GameMission.pack_tnt`) so ported code diffs cleanly |
| 2026-10-05 | **Graphics decoders (T1.3):** in `IsoDoom.Wad.Graphics` (plain C#, no Godot), so they are unit-testable; T1.6 uploads the results to the GPU. `Playpal` (any whole number of 768-byte palettes; st_stuff.c constants `STARTREDPALS`, `STARTBONUSPALS`, `RADIATIONPAL`) and `Colormap` (at least 33 maps; r_main.h `NUMCOLORMAPS`, `INVERSECOLORMAP`) keep the raw tables. Patches (`Patch`) and flats (`Flat`) decode to `IndexedImage`: a row-major `byte[]` of palette indices plus a separate row-major opacity mask, because index 0 is a real colour (black) and can't mean "transparent", and the patch `leftoffset`/`topoffset`. Patch posts are placed at their absolute `topdelta` (vanilla; tall patches are T1.3a); post pixels past the patch height are dropped; truncated headers, column offsets outside the lump and unterminated columns throw `WadFormatException`. Flats use the first 4096 bytes and reject shorter lumps. `IndexedImage.ToRgba` (palette + optional colormap) exists for debug exports and tests only. The debug PNG export is the `Doom1GraphicsTests.ExportDebugPngs` test, which writes to `TestResults/graphics/` (gitignored) with a tiny BCL-only PNG encoder in the test project, so no image library dependency |
| 2026-10-05 | **Wall textures (T1.4):** `IsoDoom.Wad.Graphics.Textures` ports r_data.c `R_InitTextures` (`PNAMES`, `TEXTURE1`, then `TEXTURE2` when present; `textures[]` keeps that order), `R_CheckTextureNumForName`/`R_TextureNumForName` (first match, case-insensitive, `-` → 0) and `R_GenerateComposite` (with `R_GenerateLookup`'s per-column patch count and `R_DrawColumnInCache`). `TextureDef`/`TexturePatch` mirror `texture_t`/`texpatch_t`. Patch origins, sizes and counts are read as signed 16-bit `short`s, as vanilla's structs do. `PNAMES` entries resolve with `W_CheckNumForName` (last lump of that name, any namespace); an unresolved entry is only an error when a texture uses it (`WadFormatException` "Missing patch in texture", vanilla's `I_Error`), since shareware's `PNAMES` lists 187 registered-only patches. A directory offset or patch list past the lump end throws. The `masked` flag is parsed but ignored, as in vanilla (shareware sets it on no texture, not even the grates). Composites are `IndexedImage`s (offsets 0) with the opacity mask as the union of drawn posts, cached per texture. Patches are clipped on all four sides. **Composite mode** (`TextureCompositeMode`, default `Vanilla`): `Vanilla` reproduces what vanilla shows. A column covered by one patch ignores the patch's `originy`, because vanilla draws those columns straight from the patch lump. In a column covered by several patches, a post clipped at the top is shortened but still copied from its first pixel (`R_DrawColumnInCache` doesn't advance `source`). In DOOM1 this changes `TEKWALL1` (top 16 rows), `BROWN144`, `SW1DIRT`/`SW2DIRT`, `AASTINKY`, `TEKWALL5` and `SKY1`. `Corrected` honours `originy` everywhere and skips the clipped pixels (Boom behaviour). This is presentation-only, so it is a loader option, not a SPEC §6.3 sim tweak. **Not modelled:** garbage in the uncovered/hole pixels of multi-patch columns (vanilla's composite buffer is uninitialised, which shows on solid walls; we keep holes transparent with index 0), the "Medusa" effect of multi-patch columns on masked midtextures (vanilla reads the composite column as posts), and "column without a patch" columns (transparent here). These are rendering bugs with no gameplay effect, and the renderer decides how solid walls use the mask (T2.3 note). Debug PNGs: `Doom1TextureTests.ExportDebugPngs` writes `TEX_*.png` to `TestResults/graphics/`. `MIDGRATE` is not in the shareware WAD, so the shareware grates `BRNSMALC`/`BRNBIGC` stood in for the masked check (T1.4a covers full IWADs) |

Sprite lumps whose prefix is not in the list are ignored and listed sprites without lumps get 0 frames (both vanilla); a custom name list can be passed. Frame lumps are `WadArchive.Lumps` indices (vanilla: `lump - firstspritelump`). Vanilla's `I_Error`s throw `WadFormatException`: bad frame/rotation characters (we also reject names that are not 6 or 8 characters), multiple rot-0 lumps, rot-0 mixed with rotations, two lumps on one rotation, a missing frame below the last, a rotated frame missing rotations. **PWAD sprite merging is per frame/rotation slot** (the intent of Chocolate Doom's w_merge.c sprite handling, settling the T1.1 note): the index is built from the raw per-file sprite lumps, not `WadArchive.GetNamespace`. Each file's lumps (a name repeated in one file collapses to its last copy) are installed into a table of their own with vanilla's checks, then laid over the earlier files' table slot by slot, so a PWAD `TROOA2` replaces only rotation 2 while rotation 8 still comes, mirrored, from the IWAD's `TROOA2A8`. A frame rotates when any of its slots came from a rotation lump (a PWAD `TROOA1` over an IWAD `TROOA0` gives a rotating frame whose other 7 slots use `TROOA0`; Chocolate Doom would `I_Error` there, Boom behaves like us); a PWAD rot-0 lump replaces all 8 slots. Conflicts *across* files are overrides, never errors. `R_InitSpriteLumps`' width/offset caches are not ported: `Patch.Decode` gives them |

So the graphic kind is exactly `GraphicLumps.FindGlobalPatches` plus the overridden copies. **Overrides** follow the archive's lookups: a namespaced lump is overridden when the merged namespace holds another lump of its name, a global lump when a later global lump has its name, a map and all its data lumps when a later map header has the map's name; markers never are. Selecting a lump that has one switches the browser's category and entry to it, without leaving the Lumps tab, so the list can be stepped through; double-click (or Enter) opens the Graphics tab. | 2026-10-06 | **Subsector polygons (T2.2):** `IsoDoom.Map.SubsectorPolygons.Build(level)` (in `IsoDoom.Map`, **integer only**, so it stays under the determinism scan rather than moving to the Godot project or a scan-excluded namespace: it is unit-testable there, the result is identical on every machine, and the renderer converts to floats once). Side tests are exact `Int128` products of fixed_t values; a corner where an edge crosses a line is rounded to the nearest fixed_t (halves away from zero). **Algorithm:** start from the vertex bounding box plus 64 units, split it along each node's partition on the way down (front to child 0, back to child 1), then clip each leaf to the front of each of its segs. Three tolerances: a corner within 1/256 unit of a clip line counts as on it (no slivers); consecutive corners within 1/1024 unit merge; and a seg whose two ends lie within 2 units of the line of an edge the cell already has (same direction) does **not** clip. Without that last rule, a seg cut between rounded split vertices and the partition along the same linedef differ by about a unit, and the seg's extended line shaves a long wedge off the cell where the neighbouring cell stops at the partition: gaps up to 2 units wide and hundreds of units long (E1M8, E1M1). The cost is up to 2 units of floor under a wall. **Output:** one convex polygon per subsector, clockwise with y up (interior on the right, like segs), as `PolygonVertex` fixed_t pairs (empty when nothing is left), and per sector the subsectors with a polygon whose `Subsector.Sector` it is (fan triangulation is the floor mesh's job). **The polygons follow the node tree** (each centroid is in its own subsector by `R_PointInSubsector`), so the floor drawn is the one the sim uses, including where the node builder's leaves are wrong: DOOM1 v1.9 E1M3 subsector 44 is floor in the void, E1M6 subsector 227 a sliver of sector 104 inside sector 20 (whether to hide or reassign those is T2.2a). **Checks** (`SubsectorPolygonTests`, `PolygonChecks`): each sector's polygon area must match its linedef area (shoelace over the line sides facing it) within 16 + 0.75 × its boundary length (rounded split vertices move edges by up to about 0.7 units), unless the sector does not close or a node artefact touches it; a scanline render compares every sample point with the sector a +x ray through the linedefs puts it in and allows no gap, overlap, or polygon in another sector or the void, except within 2.5 units of a linedef or where `R_PointInSubsector` puts the point in that very subsector (a node artefact). | 2026-10-06 | **Floor triangles (T2.4):** `IsoDoom.Map.FloorTriangles.Build(level, polygons, floorSector?)` (integer only, in `IsoDoom.Map` under the determinism scan, like T2.2/T2.3). **Three steps:** (1) **weld**: every polygon corner within 1/32 unit (`WeldEpsilon`, on both axes) of a corner already seen (subsectors in order) becomes that corner, and repeats are dropped. Neighbouring polygons compute a shared corner separately, each rounded to fixed_t, and where nearly parallel lines cross the two versions differ by up to about 1/100 unit; without welding such a near-duplicate lies within the on-edge tolerance of both edges at the other's corner, gets inserted twice, and makes the ring self-intersect (1/256 unit fails on five maps, 1/128 passes everywhere; 1/32 leaves a margin and is invisible, about 1 mm). No polygon of DOOM1 or Doom II v1.666 collapses (a collapsed one would draw nothing). (2) **T-junctions**: into each edge, every welded corner of any other polygon (any sector, hidden ones included) within 1/256 unit of the edge's line (`OnEdgeEpsilon`, the polygons' own on-line tolerance) and strictly between its ends is inserted, in order along the edge; the result is `Rings`. (3) **Triangulation by ear clipping** instead of a plain fan: an ear is a corner more than 1/256 unit off the diagonal of its neighbours whose triangle holds no other remaining corner, not even within 1/256 unit of the diagonal; the scan restarts after the first corner, so a convex polygon without inserted corners is exactly a fan from its first corner. A plain fan would leave inserted corners on the edges next to its apex (a zero-area triangle dropped there leaves the T-junction), and tolerance-collinear corners on diagonals. **Output:** per sector (`BySector`) an indexed triangle list (`SectorFloor`: corners shared within the sector, three indices per triangle), every triangle **clockwise in map space (y up)**; with Godot's usual mapping (x → +X, y → −Z) that stays clockwise seen from above, but T2.5 checks its front face after the flip. **Hook for T2.2a:** `floorSector` picks the sector whose floor a subsector's polygon is drawn in, or null to hide it; the default is `Subsector.Sector` (what the sim uses, node artefacts included). **Checks** (`FloorTriangleTests`, `FloorChecks`): the synthetic `E1M1` exactly (15 triangles, the east room's split corner inserted into the west room's edge, areas equal to the polygon and linedef areas); for every E1 map and Doom II map: every triangle strictly clockwise, each sector's triangle area equal to its rings' area exactly and to its polygons' area within the welding and insertion bound (largest drift 0.8 square units in E1, 2.5 in Doom II MAP20), every ring corner used, and no corner of any triangle in the map within 1/256 unit of any triangle edge strictly between its ends (no T-junctions, across sectors too). DOOM1 v1.9 E1M1: 692 triangles, 239 inserted corners; a build takes at most about 12 ms per map (after the polygons) |

A **texture slot** table (`texture_info`, `RGBAF`, 256 texels per row: atlas x, y, width, height) maps the slot in `CUSTOM0` to its rectangle, so animated textures and flats (M4/M5) can re-point a slot without touching meshes. So the whole level is **one `ShaderMaterial`** (one draw per sector chunk). Rejected: a material per texture (a surface per texture per chunk, many more draws, and hardware repeat could not reproduce vanilla's width mask anyway) and a `Texture2DArray` (one size for all layers: 256×128 for every 64×64 flat, and textures up to 256 wide or 128+ tall waste most of it). |

Until the game has a player (M3/M4) the position is the free-fly camera's pivot, or player 1's start under the overview camera (`--level-light-origin=X,Y` fixes it). `None`: every surface gets the colormap of one reference distance (`light_reference`, default 256 units: about the iso view's reach from the player to the screen edge), i.e. sector light only. `Camera`: the view-space depth from the rendering camera, vanilla's own distance: exactly vanilla for an eye-level perspective camera (the free-fly camera at the start of E1M1 looks as vanilla's start), but everything is far (dark) under the iso camera. Why `Player`: it keeps vanilla's tables and their look around the player (dark sectors are only readable near you, bright ones stay bright), works with any camera, and is stable. Known difference: the floor right around the player, which a vanilla view never shows (its nearest floor is ~79 units ahead), gets the tables' near entries and is full bright within 32–96 units in sectors of light 112–207; M3 tunes this with the real camera (§13 Q2). **Shader:** both tables in an R8 texture (`light_tables`, 128 × 32: rows 0–15 `zlight`, 16–31 `scalelight`), the light number per vertex (flat varying), the distance per fragment from the interpolated map position (or view depth), clamped to 4096 units and truncated to fixed_t; everything after the distance is integer, so the CPU (`LightTables.WallColormap`/`PlaneColormap`) matches it exactly. **Level check:** the light tables upload and binding, every wall quad's contrast against its side's segs, and with a renderer all pixel views compared with the CPU mapping (player distance from player 1's start; the top-down floors again with no diminishing and `extralight` 1, by camera depth from a camera half a unit off whole heights, and one tile with `colormap_override` 32), skipping pixels whose distance is within 1/64 unit of a table step; at least two sector light levels and both contrasts must be compared (synthetic: 6 levels; DOOM1 E1M1: 8 levels, 30 colormaps). Fails on a swapped contrast, walls reading `zlight`, a dropped `extralight` or a wrong distance step (windowed), and on a wrong contrast attribute (headless too). |

Walls are ignored (a floor behind a wall can be picked; the cutaway opens walls near the player anyway). Over the void it falls back to the plane at the target's floor height. W/A/S/D walk it relative to the screen (W = screen up, diagonals normalised) at vanilla's top walking speed, 264 units/s, Shift at the running one, 529 (forwardmove 25/50 under friction); floats, no collision, z the floor of `R_PointInSubsector`; it faces the cursor ground point. Script commands `mouse X Y` (viewport pixels; the headless viewport is 64×64) and `place X Y [ANGLE]`. **Level check:** every map, both projections: a point inside each sector's floor (its largest triangle's centroid) is projected through a game camera focused 96 units away and picked back; it must land on that floor within 0.5 units, or on a higher floor in front of it on the ray, and the named floor must equal `FloorTriangles.SectorAt` there (DOOM1: 2770 picks, 45 on a higher floor in front; fails when the heights are tried lowest first). |

Chosen: **vanilla's look, through geometry**: the quad is split at the origin's height, and the lower part's vertices move along their view ray towards the camera until they lie 1 unit above the thing's floor plane (`PULL_MARGIN`; at most 64 units; not when the ray is nearly horizontal), so they stay where they are on screen and pass the depth test against the floor, while still sorting against walls and things by depth. Rejected: a constant depth bias (it would pull whole sprites through nearby walls and things), a sprite pass without depth test against floors (needs a separate floor pass and breaks sorting against walls that stand on lower floors). **Sorting:** by depth alone. Masked middles (T3.1) are alpha scissor with depth writes too, so a sprite behind a fence shows through its holes and one in front covers it, in any draw order; sprites are not cut by the cutaway (T3.4), and since cut wall fragments are discarded and write no depth, a sprite behind a cut wall shows (looked at by E1M1's computer pillars: the two troopers behind the cut block appear). **Level check:** the atlas against every decoded patch (and the GPU upload), every map's billboards against a recomputed spawn list (positions, sectors, flags, and the lump and mirror for an orthographic view along the game camera and a perspective camera; kept on the CPU too, since the headless renderer stores no instance data), and with a real renderer each thing of a distinct frame, rotation and mirror alone, the level hidden, seen horizontally along the game camera's yaw at 1 unit per pixel: every pixel of its rectangle against the patch texel through the sprite light (full-bright ones with their sector darkened to 0), the rest of the frame the background; one again with no diminishing and `extralight` 1, one with a fixed colormap (it beats full bright); and one thing on open floor from the game camera's angle: the pixels of its rows below the origin must draw over the floor. On the synthetic map (imps facing all eight directions: all eight rotation slots, mirrored ones included) it fails without the mirror, the full bright, the pull, or with a contrast added. The synthetic `E1M1` gained 7 imps, a full-bright floor lamp (`COLUA0`) and a hanging body (`GOR2A0`, `MF_SPAWNCEILING`) after its first three things, and its imp patch a foot reaching 4 rows below the origin. |

Chosen: **no cap and no emulation**, as Boom/PrBoom without compatibility: the list grows, every intercept is traversed in order, and `World.interceptoverruns` counts the entries vanilla would have overrun with, so a test or a T4.8 route can tell when vanilla would have diverged. Rejected: emulating Chocolate's overrun (it models doom.exe's memory layout, only matters for demos that trigger it, and corrupts state on purpose) and truncating at 128 (neither vanilla nor sane). The blockmap quirks are kept: things are found only through the block of their centre, a start on a block boundary moves 1 unit, the walk stops after 64 blocks, and the leading 0 of each block list is walked as line 0. | 2026-10-06 | **Game loop and interpolation (T4.7, SPEC §6.1, §7.5).** **Loop:** the level scene owns one `World` per loaded map (a new game on `--level-skill` with `--level-tweaks=topdown\|vanilla`, default `Tweaks.TopDown`, on the mesh's own `Level`) and runs it from `_Process`: the frame's delta goes into a time bank and every 1/35 s banked runs one tic, `World.G_Ticker` with one `ticcmd` from `BuildTiccmd` (the player mobj's x, y, angle); at most 8 tics a frame, the rest of a long frame (a stall, a load) is dropped rather than caught up. The input is polled every frame (latches) and taken once per tic; only the game camera's input counts (the free-fly camera shares the keys): under the others the command keeps the player's angle. No tic runs without a player mobj (vanilla needs one). **Interpolation, as source ports** (Crispy Doom's `oldx`…/`interp`): `mobj_t` carries presentation-only `oldx`/`oldy`/`oldz`/`oldangle` and an `interp` flag in the sim (ints, deterministic, never read by the sim and outside the checksum); `World.P_StoreInterpolation`, the first thing `P_Ticker` does (not vanilla), copies every mobj's position and sets `interp`; `P_SpawnMobj` leaves it false, so a mobj spawned during a tic is drawn where it is, and teleports (T5.6) and the debug `World.PlaceMobj` clear it. Kept in the sim rather than as a presentation snapshot because the teleport flag must be set where the teleport happens. The presentation draws each mobj at the banked fraction of a tic between the two positions, the angle the short way (floats). The camera, light origin and cutaway follow the drawn (interpolated) player. **Drawing from mobjs:** the player mobj is drawn by its own billboard node (`PlayerSprite`, T3.3's placeholder, so `visible` can hide it alone), every other mobj by `Things` in thinker order (rebuilt when mobjs come or go; voodoo dolls are drawn, as vanilla); sprite and frame from the state, light from `subsector->sector`, the blob shadow on `floorz`. **Scripted tics:** a level script with `cmd` (raw `ticcmd`s) or `step` (tics from the input) switches the loop to running only queued tics, still paced at 35 Hz, and holding the world still while the queue is empty, so a script's checksum is the same at any `--fixed-fps` (checked at 30, 60, 144 on DOOM1 E1M1); live mode stays the game's. The visibility measure pauses the sim (animated monsters would count as the player's pixels). |
| 2026-10-06 | **The player in dark sectors (T4.7a, SPEC §7.5).** The player's sprite is lit as any thing (T3.5, T3.7), so in a light-0 sector it is drawn in colormap 31 (light 0 at the player mode's 80-unit near distance) and, with its black outline, is lost over the dark floor: DOOM1 E1M8's last room (sector 66, light 0) at (600, 6400) measured 13 % `visible`. Vanilla never draws its own player, so there is nothing to follow. Of the task's three options, a **minimum light level** is the smallest change and keeps the palette look: the sprite is drawn through the colormaps like everything else, only lit as if its sector were brighter; the room, the floor around the player and other things stay as dark as vanilla's (a light pool would brighten the floor, which changes the level's look and needs a lighting path the level shader lacks; a lighter outline alone leaves the sprite itself black). **Choice: `SpriteSettings.PlayerLight`, `--level-player-light=LEVEL\|off`, key B, default 128**, for the console player's own sprite only (`ThingSprites.Entry.Own`, instance flag 8, set by `PlayerSprite`; the shader's `own_light`): its light level is max(sector light, 128), then light diminishing, `extralight` and fixed colormaps as usual, so in sectors brighter than 128 nothing changes, and at light 0 it is drawn in colormap 12 at the near distance. **Judged** on screenshots at E1M8 (600, 6400) under lavapipe: 64 (colormap 28) only shows a dim shape; 96 (colormap 20) is readable but blends with the dark; 128 (colormap 12) shows the marine clearly while still darker than in a lit room; 160 (colormap 4) looks as in a lit room, out of place in the dark. The `visible` measure saturates at 64 (82–86 % from 64 to 160: the black outline over the black floor never counts), so it confirms the fix but cannot pick the level. With the default: 86–89 % at three points of sector 66 (13–18 % with key B off), and `tour 64` on E1M8 keeps at least 74 % everywhere (T3.8: 12–25 % in sector 66). Other players' sprites (co-op, later) and monsters are not raised. **Level check** (`LevelCheck.CheckPlayerLight`, real renderer): a lit thing alone, side-on at 1 unit per pixel, in its sector darkened to 0 with the light origin on it: marked as the player's own sprite it must draw at the minimum's colormap, at light 0's with the minimum off or unmarked, and at the sector's own light in a sector brighter than the minimum (fails with the shader rule removed, synthetic IWAD); headless, the material's `own_light` and the player billboard's own mark. |

A texture whose bottom is pegged to a plane (r_segs.c's `textureheight[]` cases) gets its anchor from the slot's height in the shader (`CUSTOM2.x` + 8, `WallSection.BottomPegged`/`TextureTopFor`), so a texture of another height anchors as vanilla. **Textures not in the atlas:** the atlas gets, up front, every texture of a *texture group* the level draws a member of: the switch pairs of `P_InitSwitchList` for the game mode (`Sim/Switches.cs`, p_switch.c's `alphSwitchList`; retail takes episode 2 as Chocolate Doom and the DOS executables, linuxdoom-1.10 leaves it at 1); T5.7 adds the animation sequences the same way (`LevelMesh.Build(textureGroups:)`). Every flat a sector floor can change to at run time is already another sector's floor (vanilla's floor specials copy floor pics), so flats need nothing more. A change to a name the atlas still lacks keeps the previous texture, warns once per name and counts in `LevelMesh.RuntimeMisses` (the level check requires 0); rejected: growing the atlas at run time (a rebuild and re-upload mid-game). A section whose texture is `-` at load has no quad, so changing it later to a texture shows nothing (vanilla's switches never do that). **p_spec.c helpers** (`World.Spec.cs`): `P_FindNextHighestFloor` ports Chocolate Doom's emulation of the DOS executables' stack overrun (the 22nd higher neighbour also overwrites the height compared against; a 23rd crashes vanilla: a `WadFormatException` here), as the vanilla reference runs it, not linuxdoom-1.10's stop at 20. **`P_ChangeSector`**/`PIT_ChangeSector`/`P_ThingHeightClip` (p_map.c) are ported whole; the crush damage calls the `P_DamageMobj` stub (T6.3), the blood and its `P_Random`s are already vanilla's. **Synthetic specials map:** the synthetic IWAD's `E1M2`, a corridor with 24 alcoves (more neighbours than `MAX_ADJOINING_SECTORS`), tags, lights and a switch pair (`SW1BRCOM`/`SW2BRCOM`, generated textures under id's names, as `TestMap`'s flat). **Debug:** the level script's `plane SECTOR floor\|ceiling HEIGHT [SPEED]` moves a plane after each tic as a mover would, `smooth FRAMES SECTOR` measures the drawn steps (even steps of 35/60 × speed at 60 fps). |

(2) else the nearest candidate by `P_AproxDistance` (ties: smaller angle off the facing, then thinker order; candidates from the thinker list, sorted by a stable insertion, no unordered collections) whose aim line along the angle to its centre finds a target (`P_AimLineAttack`: so a thing behind a wall, a ledge or out of vanilla's ±100/160 vertical window is skipped, and whatever that line hits first, e.g. a barrel in front, is what gets shot); (3) a candidate is shootable, living (`health` above 0), not a player (no co-op friendly snapping), its edge within the attack's aim range (1024 units for guns and missiles, as vanilla's aim; `MELEERANGE` for the fist and chainsaw), and has some part in the cone: its centre within the cone widened by its radius's angle as seen from the player (a centre-only cone would never snap at melee range or for things nearer than about 230 units, where vanilla's facing ray already covers ±atan(radius/distance)); barrels are candidates, as vanilla's autoaim takes them. **Where:** the guns through `P_BulletSlopeAndShoot`, which turns `mo.angle` for `P_BulletSlope` and `P_GunShot` only (both read it, kept vanilla) and turns it back, so the shotgun's pellets spread around the snapped angle and the player's facing (the drawn sprite, the next tic) is untouched; `A_Punch`/`A_Saw` start from it (their turn to the target stays vanilla); `P_SpawnPlayerMissile` starts from it, so vanilla's ±5.6° fallback tries around the snapped angle. With the tweak off `P_AimAssist` returns the facing and traces nothing (no globals touched): every vanilla route matches. **Cursor cutaway** (T3.8's note): stays off; the assist snaps only to things the aim line reaches, so it gives no reason to show monsters behind the walls in front of the cursor (a play judgement left to T6.13).
