| 2026-10-07 | **Fuzz (T6.9, §6.3 #6, §7.5).** Things with `MF_SHADOW` (spectres; the player while it has the blur sphere: the mobj's current flags) are drawn by a second pass of the sprite material (`next_pass`: `shaders/sprite_fuzz.gdshader`, the `FUZZ` variant of `sprite.gdshaderinc`, instance flag 16) in the transparent pass, which reads the screen texture (what the opaque pass drew: the level and the other sprites); the opaque pass skips them. Per opaque texel, as r_draw.c `R_DrawFuzzColumn`: the pixel one *patch row* below (`+FUZZOFF`) or above (`-FUZZOFF`) on screen (a texel stays a texel at any zoom, so the pattern is as chunky as the sprites), by vanilla's `fuzzoffset` table at `fuzzpos` = (phase + column × height + row) mod 50, matched to its nearest colour in the current palette (exact for palette colours, which is all the scene draws; the flashes and fixed colormaps therefore apply as in vanilla, where the fuzz takes priority over `fixedcolormap`) and mapped through colormap 6. Vanilla reads the row above *after* fuzzing it, so runs of `-FUZZOFF` darken again per row (up to 5 times): ported (`Fuzz.Source`), it makes vanilla's darker streaks. Vanilla's `fuzzpos` runs on across frames; here the phase is `leveltime` × 23 mod 50 (`Fuzz.Phase`), deterministic, shimmering per tic. Not ported: the view border clamp (`dc_yl`/`dc_yh`). **Outline:** a fuzzed thing has none (vanilla's silhouette), except the player's own sprite, which keeps its solid outline (lit with its minimum light, T4.7a) so the player can find themselves with the blur sphere (vanilla's first person shows the fuzzed weapon); the minimum light does not touch the fuzz itself. **Limits:** fuzzed things do not see each other (not in the screen texture), and with the upright hiding (T3.6a) all sprites are drawn in the transparent pass, so a fuzzed thing shows the level but not the sprites behind it. `--level-fuzz=on|off` (`SpriteSettings.Fuzz`; off draws them as any sprite). The level check compares the drawn fuzz with the CPU reference pixel by pixel (`CheckFuzz`). |
| 2026-10-07 | **Sim events (T6.10, §6.1, §7.7).** Vanilla's game code calls the sound and HUD code directly; the sim queues output-only events instead (`World.events`, `sim_event_t`: `se_startsound`, `se_stopsound`, `se_message`), which nothing in the sim reads, so the checksum cannot depend on a listener (tested per tic along routes). **Lifetime:** the presentation drains the queue after each tic (`DrainEvents`); at the start of each tic (`P_Ticker`) the sim drops what is left from earlier tics (events are stamped with the `leveltime` they belong to), so an absent listener costs one tic's worth of memory, and an event made between tics (a route's damage, the level script) belongs to the next tic. **Sounds:** every `S_StartSound` call is queued, before vanilla's audibility check (`S_AdjustSoundParams`, the `sfx->link` volume): distance, channels and priorities are the presentation's (T7.7). | 2026-10-07 | **Door lids (T6.13b, SPEC §7.2, §7.3; a deviation from "ceilings are never drawn").** Found in T6.13a's hand play (DOOM1 E1M1, west of door sector 4): with no ceilings a closed door is two upper walls 16 units apart with a hollow slot between them, and the cutaway cuts its camera-facing side to a 32-unit stub that reads as a step. **Which sectors** (`IsoDoom.Render.DoorLids`, plain C#, `DoorLidsTests`): a sector whose ceiling is not the sky, with a floor and at least one other sector across a two-sided line, and at most 32 units thick (`MaxThickness`: twice the radius of the largest disc in its floor, sampled on a 1-unit grid), or 64 when it is closed at load (ceiling at or below its floor: Doom II's 64-thick doors, MAP17, MAP27). The task asked for every sector whose ceiling is below its neighbours'; without the thickness limit rooms with a low ceiling (DOOM1 E1M1's sector 74, about 190 thick) would get a ceiling hiding what is in them; 32 is two player radii, so nothing stands wholly under a lid. Over DOOM1 and Doom II this takes 611 of the 642 sectors closed at load below their neighbours' ceilings (the other 31 are 70–160-thick closets and rooms that open, whose lid would hide what comes out), and the lintels over open doorways, windows and thin low passages: 5–192 lid sectors a map (DOOM1: 26–134); loading stays far under SPEC §9's 1 s (slowest Doom II map 291 ms headless). **Height:** the lowest ceiling among its neighbours (all of them: a sky neighbour's too, since a non-sky lid sector's upper walls are drawn on every side), drawn while the sector's own ceiling is below it: every upper wall around it reaches that high, so the lid closes the box they make (a higher neighbour's upper wall stands above it). A door that opens rises to 4 below it (p_doors.c), so the lid stays as the lintel's 4-unit top; it follows moving neighbours. **Drawing:** each lid sector's floor triangles once more in its chunk (vertex kind 5, `LevelMesh.KindLid`), placed by the vertex shader from a new RG float data texture `sector_lids` (lid height from the drawn, interpolated ceilings, `LevelMesh.UpdateSectors`; the ceiling flat's slot) and collapsed while the sector's ceiling is at or above it. **What it shows:** the sector's ceiling flat (added to the atlas; its underside, so the door reads as one material), lit as a floor of the sector (its light, the light diminishing), not darkened: compared on E1M1's doors with the dark cap next to it, the ceiling flat reads as the block's top and the darker cap as its cut. The upper texture's top row (no texture coordinates across the lid) and a flat colour (out of the palette's look) were rejected. **Cutaway:** a lid is cut as any floor above the cutoff; the cap (T3.4a, kind 4) also caps the solid under a lid where the sector's ceiling is at or below the cutoff and the lid above it (a closed door), showing the lid's flat at the cutoff, so a cut door reads as a 32-unit block with a dark top. **Option:** `DoorLidMode` (`--level-door-lids=on\|off`, key J), on by default, under every camera: a lid faces up, and from inside a neighbour the upper walls around it hide it, so vanilla comparisons from eye level (`tools/VanillaRef/compare.sh`) are unchanged. **Looked at** (lavapipe, game camera): DOOM1 E1M1's doors (sector 4 from both sides, opening, the player walking through it, closing again; (2912–2944, −3904 to −3776); (2976–3040, −4648 to −4632) from both sides), E1M2's red key door (lines 527/528), Doom II MAP01's doors: closed doors read as blocks with a top, cut ones as 32-unit blocks with a dark top, open ones keep a thin lintel. **Visibility** (`tour 32`, lids on): DOOM1 E1M1 (654 steps) at least 81 % of the player visible, as with the lids off, 91–93 % standing inside a door (97–99 % off: the lid's cut edge); Doom II MAP01 (59 sector floors) passes the default 25 %. **Level check:** every map's lid texels (recomputed from the level, also with the lowest neighbour's ceiling moved up and down) and lid vertices; with a real renderer a closed door's lid straight from above (its ceiling flat, the rest as with the lids off) and along the game camera from 24 units behind it (the cutaway check's classes with the lid planes as surfaces: whole, cut, and capped with the ceiling flat), then with the door half open (ceiling above the cutoff: the lid stays, nothing capped). It fails with the lid 4 units off, the floor flat on the lid or its cap, or no cap under a lid. The synthetic E1M1's door D has a lid at 128 (room C's ceiling), hidden from the game camera by courtyard A's 256-unit upper wall, as in vanilla: CI compares it top-down and cut. **Not done:** highlighting the line the use action would trigger (the task's optional part): a T8.5 note. |

Vanilla's volumes stay on its Sound Volume menu, the messages, detail and screen size on its options (also on the HUD page). **The settings** (`src/Game/Settings.cs`, plain C#): window size (windowed; fullscreen covers the screen), fullscreen (off, borderless `on`, `exclusive`), vsync (on, off, adaptive), frame cap (`Engine.MaxFps`, off or 30–240), zoom, pitch and projection (the game camera's), aim assist (0–45°, the sim's `Tweaks.AimAssistCone`: `World.tweaks` is no longer read-only, the option changes it between tics; a lockstep game would send the change with the tic's commands), the cutaway (style, things, cap, radius, cursor), light diminishing (SPEC §8's "authentic vs enhanced lighting"), door lids, masked backs, weapon light, palette flashes (T6.8's photosensitivity note), the sprites' readability (tilt, tilt depth, shadows, outline, wall pull, upright hiding, player light, fuzz), the HUD (mode, scale, messages, detail), mouse sensitivity, the two volumes, and every input action's bindings. Each value is in its `--level-*` argument's form, parsed by the same code. **Applied at once:** the menu changes the scene's state (the scene stays the truth); every frame the scene's values are compared with the saved ones, so vanilla's options, the debug keys (L, X, …), Ctrl+wheel and O are saved too. **Saved** to `user://settings.cfg` (WadLocator's file, `[wad]` kept), only what differs from its default (so a changed default reaches users who never touched it), as strings: `[video] zoom="720"`, `[controls] attack="key:70 padaxis:5+"` (Godot's physical key codes, mouse and pad button numbers, a pad axis with its direction). **Command line:** a `--level-*` argument wins over the file for the run and is not saved unless the setting changes during the run (`SettingValues.Pin`); `--level-tweaks` pins the aim assist (vanilla's tweaks keep it off). `--settings=FILE|off` picks the file; headless runs, the checks, `--level-script`, `--level-screenshot` and the viewer's checks run on the defaults and write nothing unless `--settings=FILE` names one, so the user's settings never change a check's result. **Not applied headless or under the level check:** the window size, fullscreen and vsync (recorded only). **Volumes:** two audio buses, `Sfx` and `Music` (T7.7 and T7.8 play on them), at vanilla's 0–15 as a share of full amplitude (0 mutes). **Mouse sensitivity:** g_game.c's mouse turning, ported for the vanilla tweaks (`TiccmdInput.MouseX`, `mousex = dx * (mouseSensitivity + 5) / 10`, `angleturn -= mousex * 8`), without its forward motion (Chocolate Doom's `novert`); the twin-stick game aims at the cursor, which no sensitivity changes. **Rebinding:** the game's actions (`project.godot`'s, T4.6) and, new, the menus' (`menu_open`, `menu_up`/`down`/`left`/`right`, `menu_select`, `menu_back`, `menu_yes`, `menu_no`: Chocolate Doom's `key_menu_*` defaults plus the pad's Start, D-pad, left stick, A and B); the menus read their keys from these actions (the pad's directions polled from the actions' pad inputs, for the repeat; yes and no are their own codes, so a rebound Y is only a letter; typing a save's description takes the keys as they are). Binding an input replaces the action's inputs of the same kind (keyboard and mouse, or pad) and takes it from the other actions of its group (the game's, or the menus'); the menus wait for the input (Escape cancels, or 8 s for a pad), Delete clears the keyboard's and mouse's inputs, the pad's X the pad's. A key bound to a game action is no longer a debug key. **Function keys (F1–F11) not ported:** F1 and F3 stay the debug overlay's; help, save, load, volume and the others are in the menus (T7.6 adds quicksave and quickload as rebindable actions). |

| 2026-10-07 | **Save and load (T7.6, SPEC §8).** **Format: our own, exact** (`World.SaveG.cs`, `SaveGameFile`), not vanilla's `doomsav*.dsg`: vanilla's p_saveg.c drops state the sim reads from tic to tic (the `P_Random` index; the mobjs' `target`/`tracer`, which it nulls; the sector and block lists, rebuilt in thinker order, so `PIT_CheckThing` meets things in another order; `onground`; buttons' sound origins) and its loads go another way than the game saved. Ours keeps every field the sim reads between tics, so a loaded game is the saved game: the same checksum on every tic after, and the same bytes saved again (tested on every DOOM1 exit and fight route and demo, and the synthetic and test map routes). **References** are indices: thinkers into a table written before their fields (the thinker list in order, then the thinkers only referenced, found while writing: a mobj removed but still a missile's `target`, as C# keeps it alive where vanilla's `Z_Free`d memory still held it), sectors, lines and subsectors into the level's arrays, players into the world's. **Load** (`P_UnArchiveGame`): a new world with the save's settings (skill, game mode, `-nomonsters`, …) and tweaks on a fresh copy of the map, only the map part of `P_SetupLevel` (`P_SetupLevelMap`): no things spawned, no specials started, no `P_Random` drawn; vanilla loads the level whole (`G_InitNew`) and throws its thinkers away. Not saved (rebuilt or transient): `validcount`s (all below the new world's), the presentation's interpolation (drawn where it is), the event queue, the intermission's `wminfo`, the play globals set before every use (`tmthing`, `linetarget`, intercepts, …). **File:** magic `ISODOOMSAVE`, `VERSION` (1; bumped whenever saved state or its layout changes: no reading of older versions, refused with a message), the description, map, game mode, skill and `leveltime` (the menus read them without the WAD), the state, a 64-bit FNV-1a hash. **Refused with a message** (the menus' message box, as vanilla's other refusals; vanilla returns silently on another version): no save, an older or a newer version (checked before the hash), damaged (hash, cut short, data out of range, the state's map not the header's), another game's (another game mode; a map the WAD lacks or whose sectors, lines, sides, blockmap or animations differ). A load is tried on a fresh copy of the map first, so a refused one leaves the game shown as it is. **Where:** one directory per IWAD, as Chocolate Doom's `savegamedir` (`user://savegames/doom1.wad/isodoomsav0.dsg`…); headless runs, the checks, the level script and screenshots save to a temporary directory of their own unless `--savedir=DIR` (as the settings, T7.3). **Flow as vanilla:** the menus' save goes with the next tic (`sendsave`: its buttons become `BT_SPECIAL \| BTS_SAVEGAME \| slot`, the player's other buttons lost for that tic, as vanilla's) and saves after it (`ga_savegame`; an exit or reborn the same tic wins, as vanilla's), only on a level of a user game; `GGSAVED`; a load runs `G_InitNew`'s part (unpaused, `M_ClearRandom`, a user game, its skill) and `G_DoLoadLevel`'s forced wipe, so it melts from the title or over a level. The level script's `save` saves at once between tics (no tic needed). **Tweaks:** the save's (T7.3's note: the aim assist's cone can change during a game; a lockstep replay needs the game's), but in the game scene the aim assist's cone is the option's when the aiming tweak matches (the option applies at once during a game, so a loaded game follows it too). **Quicksave and quickload** (m_menu.c's, Chocolate Doom's keys F6 and F9, rebindable on the Actions page as the input actions `quicksave`/`quickload`, read when the menus are closed as vanilla's F-keys; the other function keys stay unported, T7.3). **Pad default description (not vanilla):** a pad can't type, so an empty slot chosen with A (or the left button) starts with the map and its level time (`E1M3 2:05`), which A saves; the keyboard can still edit it. |
| 2026-10-07 | **Sound effects (T7.7, SPEC §7.7).** **Ported as vanilla** (plain C#, `src/Audio/SSound.cs`, linked into the tests): s_sound.c as Chocolate Doom has it: 8 channels, `S_GetChannel`'s priorities (sounds.c `S_sfx`, now in `src/Sim/Sounds.cs`: a lower number wins; with every channel busy a sound takes the first whose priority is no better, else it is dropped), a new sound from an origin stopping its last one (`S_StopSound(origin)` before `S_GetChannel`, also for no origin: the menus' sounds replace each other), `S_AdjustSoundParams` (clipped beyond 1200 units, full within 200, then linear to 1200; map 8 of any episode heard everywhere at 15 or more; the separation `128 − 96·sin`), `S_UpdateSounds` every frame (channels follow their mobjs and the listener), `S_Start` at every level load (a reborn too). Origins are the events' (T6.10): a mobj, a sector's `soundorg`, or none (pickups, the switch press while `buttonlist[0]` is free, the menus, the intermission and the finale: full volume, centre); a removed mobj's `se_stopsound` stops its sound, as vanilla's `P_RemoveMobj` (an explosion's sound ends with its mobj, as vanilla's). **Switch sounds:** kept at vanilla's origins (T5.4's, T6.10's question): the press with no origin (full volume, centre) while `buttonlist[0]` is free, which is where the player stands anyway, else from `buttonlist[0]`'s sector as vanilla's; the release from the button line's front sector (vanilla's is garbage). Placing them at the switch would need the line in the event; T7.7a listens whether it is worth it. **Positional audio: vanilla's attenuation and separation, not Godot's 3D audio:** the same distances, priorities and channel use as vanilla, testable without the engine; the distance is from the player as vanilla's, but the separation's "ahead" is the camera's screen-up direction (the game camera's: map north-west), so a sound on the left of the screen is heard on the left whatever the player faces (twin-stick aiming turns the player all the time; vanilla's view is its facing, ours is the screen). `--level-sound-stereo=screen|facing|off` (`facing` is vanilla's; a command-line option until T7.7b). No front/back distinction, as vanilla's. **Mixing:** each channel an `AudioStreamPlayer` on a bus of its own (`Sfx0`–`Sfx7`, an `AudioEffectPanner`), sending to `Sfx`: the panner's linear law at half amplitude (−6 dB) gives Chocolate Doom's `I_SDL_UpdateSoundParams` gains (`(254 − sep)·vol/127` and `sep·vol/127` of 255) exactly; the channel's volume is its share of `snd_SfxVolume` (`sfxVolume · 8`, computed as vanilla's so the same sounds are inaudible), the `Sfx` bus applies the options' volume (T7.3's `volume / 15`, 0.49 dB above vanilla's `sfxVolume · 8 / 127` at every step: kept). Samples play at their own rate (Godot resamples; vanilla v1.9 had no pitch variation, none here). **Decoding:** i_sdlsound.c `CacheSFX`: DMX's 16 pad bytes at each end skipped, lumps of 48 samples or fewer or with a count beyond the lump not played. **Time:** each channel counts its sound's length down itself (frame time, not the driver's), standing in for `I_SoundIsPlaying`, so channel use and priorities do not depend on the audio driver (Godot's dummy driver under `--headless` included); the driver is told to stop a channel whose count ran out. **Pause:** the game's pause is vanilla's `S_PauseSound` (the music only, T7.8; the sound effects play out); losing the window's focus holds every channel. **Quit:** the menu's Quit waits vanilla's `I_WaitVBL(105)` (1.5 s) with nothing running, and no later menu sound (vanilla never returns from `M_QuitResponse`). The level script and the level check stop the sounds and let the audio server free them before quitting (Godot reports playbacks still active at exit as leaked). Not checked against the vanilla reference: doomgeneric's reference has no sound driver, so its channels say nothing; the event sequence is vanilla's (T6.10) and the channel logic is a line-by-line port. |
| 2026-10-07 | **GENMIDI and MUS lumps (T7.8b, SPEC §7.7).** **Readers** in plain C# in `src/Wad` (`Genmidi.cs`, `MusSong.cs`, beside `DmxSound.cs`), keeping the C's names where they are its (`genmidi_instr_t`'s fields, `main_instrs`, `percussion_instrs`, `GENMIDI_FLAG_*`) and raw values (no decoding of the OPL register bits: the driver writes them as they are). **GENMIDI:** i_oplmusic.c checks the header only and reads past a short lump; here a lump too short for the 175 instruments is refused, and the names (which the driver never reads) are taken where the lump has them (`HasNames` false otherwise) rather than refusing the lump. **MUS:** read as mus2mid.c reads it, not as DMX does: from `scorestart` to the score end whatever the header's score length says (every Doom and Doom II song's score end is the last byte of its score anyway), delays as an unsigned 32-bit number wrapping as the C's; refused only where mus2mid.c fails on a well-formed read too (the lump ending before the score end, inside an event or a delay) or where the MUS format has no meaning (type 7). What the MUS format allows but mus2mid.c refuses (a measure end, type 5; a system event outside 10–14; a controller above 9) is read and named in `MusSong.Mus2MidError`, so the viewer shows such a song and the driver (T7.8d) can refuse it as `I_OPL_RegisterSong` would. **Events keep their bytes** (descriptor, two data bytes, offset) with their absolute time and group delay, so T7.8d's port of mus2mid.c can run on them or on the lump (`MusSong.Data`) and convert exactly. **Lump list:** a song's detail is its events, length in tics and seconds at 140 Hz, and channels; the bank's its counts; the info line on selection lists a song's header and events or every instrument by program or percussion note. **Synthetic IWAD:** its `GENMIDI` and `D_E1M1` are now generated instruments and a generated song (formerly a silent bank and a two-note song), so T7.8d's synthetic log has something to play. |

a level with no song in the table stops the music; the focus pause (`SSound.PauseAll`) holds the song too, and a new song started under it; `S_Start`'s `mus_paused = 0` also lets go of a song the pause held when the level's is the same (vanilla's would stay paused, the unpause then a no-op: unreachable in practice, a level load being unpaused first). The menu's music volume reaches `S_SetMusicVolume(musicVolume * 8)` from the scene each frame it changed; the `Music` bus keeps the options' volume too until T7.8e decides. **Without demos** the title loop asks for its song once a round and it goes on (vanilla's demos play their levels' songs in between): checked against the call sites, not a vanilla run. **Checked:** `MusicTests` against `tools/VanillaRef/wipes.sh`'s `NAME.music` (dump.c's `$DUMP_MUSIC`, ref.patch's hook at `S_ChangeMusic`'s entry: every call's gametic, game state, song and looping). |

The timer registers (2, 3, 4) stay off the chip as opl_sdl.c keeps them; the chip detection (timer writes only) is not ported. **Floats:** MetaSetTempo's `OPL_AdjustCallbacks((float) us_per_beat / tempo)` (MIDI files only; mus2mid.c writes no tempo) is computed in single precision with `(uint64_t)` of a float as x86-64 GCC compiles it (negative values wrap, NaN gives 2^63; .NET's conversion saturates). **Default OPL3** (18 voices, OPL3 panning), as the task asked: Chocolate Doom's own default `snd_dmxoption` is empty (OPL2) unless its setup's "OPL3" is chosen (`-opl3`); `opl3: false` gives OPL2 (T7.8g's option). Doom 1.9's `opl_drv_ver`; `-reverse` (`opl_stereo_correct`) off. **The music volume** is i_oplmusic.c's: it changes the notes' levels (a voice's carrier and, in additive mode, modulator), and starts at 0 until `I_OPL_SetMusicVolume` (s_sound.c's `S_Init` sets it). **Not vanilla (undefined in the C):** a MIDI velocity above 127 reads the volume table's last value (the C reads past it), program changes 128–255 read the bank's bytes as the C's pointer would (percussion instruments, then names), bytes past the lump read as 0, and a MIDI file of time division 0 is refused (the C would divide by zero). **Reference:** `tools/OplRef/music_ref.c` links Chocolate Doom's unmodified sources on a stub SDL (`stub/`: no mutexes, SDL_mixer's post-mix effect called by the harness, `SDL_CondWait` running the mixer so `OPL_Delay`'s detection wait passes without clocking the chip) and logs every `OPL3_WriteRegBuffered` with its output sample (opl_sdl.c compiled with it and `OPL3_GenerateStream` renamed to the harness's); `music.sh` writes the synthetic IWAD's runs (committed, with their SHA-256 in `logs/SHA256SUMS`, checked by `check.sh` against both C chips) and any WAD's songs' logs into `~/.cache/isodoom/opl-music/`. **Checked:** every song of DOOM1.WAD and doom2.wad, whole and looped once (2.7 million writes), the same writes at the same samples; five songs a minute each bit for bit; the synthetic runs (OPL2, OPL3, the v1.666 drivers, pause, volume, song changes, a type 1 MIDI with tempo changes) in CI. **Cost** (Release, desktop): 2.6–2.9% of a core for D_E1M1 at 48 kHz. |
| 2026-10-07 | **Music playback (T7.8e, SPEC §7.7).** **Player:** `src/Audio/MusicPlayer.cs`, a `Node` and the game's `IMusicDevice` (made once in `LevelScene.InitSound`, for the game's life; the viewer makes one per WAD), holds one `OplMusic` at `AudioServer.GetMixRate()` (OPL3, Doom 1.9's driver) and plays it through an `AudioStreamGenerator` (an `AudioStreamPlayer` on the `Music` bus). **Thread:** a dedicated background thread renders steady 512-frame blocks (`OPL_Mix_Callback`) whenever the generator's ring buffer has room for one, else sleeps 2 ms, and pushes them itself (`PushBuffer`), so the game's frames (a slow one, a level load of up to 1 s) never feed or starve it; the generator's ring buffer is the ring buffer (no second one). The chip renders on when no song plays, so a stop or a pause is the chip's own key-offs and release envelopes: no click. **Lock:** one lock around every game-thread call forwarded to the driver (`I_RegisterSong` … `I_SetMusicVolume`, mus2mid.c's conversion included: well under a millisecond) and around each block's render; no command queue. Every call is also recorded (`MusicPlayer.Record`, T7.8c's `RecordingMusicDevice`), so the level check and the overlay keep reading the device's state. A WAD without `GENMIDI`: no driver, every song refused (silence, `SSound` counts it as playing). **Ring buffer:** `DefaultBufferSeconds` 60 ms asked, but at least two output-driver periods and two blocks (`DriverMinimumSeconds`): Godot's dummy driver (headless) mixes 4096 frames at once every 93 ms, so a 93 ms buffer ran dry at every mix (44 underruns a second); there it is 371 ms (16384 frames at 44.1 kHz), under PulseAudio (Xvfb, 32 ms output latency) 93 ms (4096 frames; Godot rounds to a power of two). `--level-music-buffer=MS` asks for more (T7.8g may make it an option). **Underruns** are the generator's skips (mixes that found it short) since the buffer was first full; the overlay's `music:` line shows them with the buffer's level and the render's cost, and the level script's `underruns [MAX]` checks them. **Volume (decided):** vanilla's, i_oplmusic.c's `I_OPL_SetMusicVolume` (`SSound`'s `musicVolume * 8`: the notes' levels, so the mix and the instruments' balance change as on DOS), with the `Music` bus at unity; the bus mutes only at 0, since the driver's 0 leaves notes at the chip's −47 dB (a faint song), not silence (not vanilla). The sound effects' bus keeps T7.3's share of amplitude. **Stops without clicks:** pause, focus loss, level changes and loads go through the driver (key-offs, `AllNotesOff`), as Chocolate Doom's; every quit (`QuitQuietly`, the menu's Quit after its sound, the window's close: the scene turns `AutoAcceptQuit` off and quits as the script does, a second close at once; the checks' ends) stops the song, fades the output over 20 ms, waits for the buffer to be heard out, then stops the thread and lets the stream's playback go (else Godot reports it leaked); the scene's `_ExitTree` stops the thread whatever quits. **Cost** (measured on the thread, the render of each block under the lock, i7-11850H): the exported release build 6–9% of a core (D_E1M1 about 7%, Doom II's D_RUNNIN 8–9%), above T7.8d's 2.6–2.9% benchmark, likely because the bursty thread wakes on a clocked-down core; the editor's Debug build (unoptimized JIT) 26–50%, up to 74% under lavapipe's load, still without an underrun. **Checked:** a 5-minute script (title 30 s, a new game, the pause, a save and a load, E1M2 and E1M3 by PgDn, the pause again) with 0 underruns headless (300 s rendered) and offscreen under Xvfb with PulseAudio (93 ms buffer); the level check's 3 s song on DOOM1, Doom II and the synthetic IWAD (rendered at the mix rate, notes heard, no underrun; a WAD without `GENMIDI` silent); the viewer check plays every `D_*` and hears one for a second. Not heard by a person yet: T7.8f. |
| 2026-10-07 | **Music options (T7.8g, SPEC §7.7).** **Option:** `sound/opl` (`opl3`, the default as T7.8e's, or `opl2`, Chocolate Doom's default and the AdLib's 9 voices), on a new More Options page **Sound** (menu name `soundsetup`, since vanilla's volume menu is `sound`; it shows the two volumes too, and takes T7.7b's options), saved, `--level-opl=opl3|opl2` pinning it for a run. **Switch (decided):** the mode is fixed when a driver is made (Chocolate Doom reads `snd_dmxoption` at `I_OPL_InitMusic`), so a switch makes a new `OplMusic` (off the lock: it reads only the bank), and under the render thread's lock gives it the volume and the song the game last played (`midi_file_t` is the driver's input, not its state, so the same one plays on the new driver) from its start, looping as asked and paused if it was; a song stopped, or ended without looping (`RunningTracks` 0), stays silent. The old driver is keyed off (`I_OPL_StopSong`) and renders on beneath the new one, faded linearly over 30 ms, then dropped (`OplMusicMixer`, plain C#): its output is continuous into the fade, the new chip starts from silence, so no click and no stuck note; a second switch inside the fade fades both. Not the song's position carried over (vanilla has no seek; a restart is what a new `I_PlaySong` does). **Buffer size:** no option; `--level-music-buffer` stays an argument until T7.8f or T8.1 hears a dropout. **Render cost, T7.8e's open point (measured):** not the JIT or the build: the same tier-1 driver code renders a 512-frame block of D_E1M1 (44.1 kHz) at 2.1% of a core back to back, 2.2% when paced by a busy wait, and 7.8% when paced by a 10 ms sleep as the music thread is (Release, i7-11850H, `powersave` governor). The overlay times each block on the wall clock just after the thread wakes from its sleep, on a core clocked down and cold, so it reads 3–4× the work; the CPU spent is that of an idle core's clock, not a cost to fix. Batching more blocks per wake or busy-waiting would only move the number (and waste power); kept as is. |

| 2026-10-08 | **Purity test: no Godot reference in the libraries (Q2, docs/CODE-QUALITY.md).** `tests/IsoDoom.Tests/Purity/GodotPurityTests.cs` enforces SPEC §4's rule that Wad, Map and Sim never reference Godot, in two layers, because the compiler drops an assembly reference no code uses: (1) the compiled metadata, read with Mono.Cecil (assembly references, type and member references, defined types, assembly and module attributes), fails on any name or namespace starting with `Godot` (`GodotSharp`, `GodotSharpEditor`, `Godot.SourceGenerators`, …); (2) the test's own `IsoDoom.Tests.deps.json`, which lists each project's package and project dependencies whether used or not, fails on a `Godot*` anywhere in a library's dependency closure, so a bare `PackageReference` is caught too. **Sim's allow-list:** Sim may reference only the BCL and `IsoDoom.Map`/`IsoDoom.Wad`, both in its metadata and in its deps.json closure; "BCL" means an assembly whose `NAME.dll` lies in the running shared framework's directory (`RuntimeEnvironment.GetRuntimeDirectory()`), so it needs no hand-kept list of `System.*` names, and any other dependency must be added to `_simAllowedReferences` in the test, as a conscious decision. Checked: a `GodotSharp` `PackageReference` added to `IsoDoom.Sim.csproj` fails the deps.json and allow-list tests, and a `typeof(Godot.Node)` in Sim also fails the metadata test. |
| 2026-10-08 | **`actionlint` in CI and up-to-date actions (Q6, docs/CODE-QUALITY.md).** **Actions** bumped to their current majors, each checked to declare `runs.using: node24` in its `action.yml` at that tag: `actions/checkout` v5 → **v7**, `actions/setup-dotnet` v5 → **v6**, `actions/cache` v5 → **v6**, `actions/upload-artifact` v5 (`node20`, the deprecation warning) → **v7**. Their release notes list nothing this workflow depends on: checkout v6 keeps persisted credentials in a file under `$RUNNER_TEMP` instead of `.git/config` and v7 refuses fork pull request code under `pull_request_target`/`workflow_run` (we use neither, and never push from CI); setup-dotnet v6 and cache v6 only move to ESM and new `@actions/*` packages; upload-artifact v6 moved to Node.js 24 and v7 adds `archive: false` for single unzipped files (the default stays a zip, as before). All need runner ≥ 2.327.1, which GitHub's hosted runners have. **Dependabot** (`.github/dependabot.yml`, `github-actions`, weekly, commits prefixed `CI`) proposes later bumps; NuGet, the SDK, Godot and actionlint stay pinned by hand. **Lint job:** `actionlint` (no `needs`, so it runs alongside the build) installs the dev container's actionlint, whose version and SHA-256 it reads from `.devcontainer/Dockerfile`'s `ARG ACTIONLINT_VERSION=`/`ARG ACTIONLINT_SHA256=` lines (one place for both; the Dockerfile says so), checks the tarball's SHA-256 and runs `actionlint` from the repo root. shellcheck comes from the runner image (ubuntu-24.04 ships 0.9.0, the dev container's version too); the job prints its version first, so a runner without it fails rather than having actionlint skip the scripts silently. **Known gap:** actionlint 1.7.12, the latest release, knows the inputs of `checkout` up to v6, `cache` and `setup-dotnet` up to v5 and `upload-artifact` up to v7, so it does not check the `with:` keys of `checkout@v7`, `cache@v6` and `setup-dotnet@v6` (a misspelt input passes); everything else (expressions, contexts, job graph, shellcheck) is checked. Bump actionlint when a release adds them. |
| 2026-10-08 | **`.editorconfig` and a one-time reformat (Q3, docs/CODE-QUALITY.md).** A repo-root `.editorconfig` encodes the [Godot C# style guide](https://docs.godotengine.org/en/stable/tutorials/scripting/c_sharp/c_sharp_style_guide.html) over Microsoft's [.NET coding conventions](https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/dotnet/csharp/fundamentals/coding-style/coding-conventions) (both read for it; Godot's wins where they differ). **Files:** UTF-8 without BOM, LF, a final newline, trailing whitespace trimmed, 4 spaces (C#, scripts, C, Python), 2 for MSBuild/JSON/YAML, tabs for `.gdshader`/`.gdshaderinc`/`.gd` (Godot's shading and GDScript guides; our shaders already use tabs). Exceptions: `.sln` stays CRLF with tabs, as Visual Studio and `dotnet sln` write it (rewriting it to LF would churn on every `dotnet sln add`); `.csproj` enforces no final newline either way (Godot's guide exempts csproj from its final newline because Godot writes them without one; ours have one); `.patch` keeps trailing whitespace (`tools/VanillaRef/ref.patch`'s context lines need it). **C#:** Allman braces with `else`/`catch`/`finally` on new lines, Roslyn's default spacing (spaces around binary operators, none after a cast), `case` blocks indented (Roslyn's default; a few blocks moved), `max_line_length = 100` for editors only (Godot's recommendation; Roslyn can't enforce it, so the ~2,200 longer lines stay); `System` usings first, outside the namespace, file-scoped namespaces and unused usings removed (IDE0065, IDE0161, IDE0005 at **warning**, so CI enforces them); `var` only when the type is apparent and explicit types for built-in types (Godot: `var` when evident from the right side, numbers explicit), Godot's modifier order, keywords over BCL type names, no `this.`, explicit accessibility; everything else (expression-bodied members, pattern matching, null checks, collection expressions) keeps the IDE defaults. Godot's guide asks for C# 12 (its .NET 8 baseline); this project builds with .NET 10 and `LangVersion` `latest` (`src/Directory.Build.props`, `IsoDoom.csproj`), so the fixes may use newer features (the reformat brought C# 14's null-conditional assignment, `node?.Visible = false`, outside Sim). **Turned off:** primary constructors (IDE0290: their parameters are mutable captures, not the readonly fields they replace); IDE0130 (namespace matches folder) under `src/{Assets,Audio,Game,Render}`, because the Godot project's root is the repo root while its namespaces start at `src/`; every style rule in `tests/IsoDoom.Tests/Purity/ScannerFixtures.cs`, whose constructs are the purity scanner's test input (a style fix turned its lambda into a static local function). **Naming:** Godot's (PascalCase types, methods, properties, events, constants and non-private fields; `_camelCase` private fields; camelCase locals and parameters; `I` interfaces) at **suggestion** until Q4; nothing was renamed. **Generated code** (`generated_code = true`): `.godot/`, `export/`, `bin/`, `obj/` and `doom_20230531/` (a downloaded archive of the original game, already gitignored); `tools/VanillaRef` and `tools/OplRef` track only our own files (they fetch doomgeneric, Nuked-OPL3 and Chocolate Doom into `~/.cache`), so nothing there is marked. **Reformat** (one commit, in `.git-blame-ignore-revs`): `dotnet format whitespace` then `dotnet format style --severity info` (the Godot project formats under `dotnet format` like the others), 185 files, ±1,300 lines, mostly collection expressions (`new[] { … }`, `Array.Empty<T>()`, `.ToArray()` → `[…]`), `var`/explicit types, sorted usings. **Not applied**, as not pure style or as reshaping ported code (vanilla's statement shapes keep it diffable): unused parameters (IDE0060: ported functions keep vanilla's parameter lists, and removing them changes signatures), dead stores (IDE0059: it deleted vanilla's `hitline = true` in `PTR_ShootTraverse`), switch statements to switch expressions (IDE0066: one-line `or` chains of up to 60 cases), tuple swaps (IDE0180), deconstruction (IDE0042: it named locals `Left`, `Top`), foreach's explicit casts (IDE0220: it broke a build over a Godot array), and in `src/Sim` null propagation (IDE0031: `if (thing.snext != null) thing.snext.sprev = …` stays as vanilla's). These stay at their IDE default, `suggestion`, which neither the build nor CI's `--severity warn` applies or fails on. Sim's diff is only collection expressions for its tables (still RVA data for the `ReadOnlySpan` ones), `finesine[(FINEANGLES / 4)..]` for `.Slice`, two `var`s on casts, one tuple type for a `var` and whitespace; no arithmetic, cast, `checked`/`unchecked` or evaluation order changed. Checked: every test passes (1,669 with the DOOM1 dumps of all `tools/VanillaRef` scripts and the OPL references built; route, demo, wipe, intermission, finale and music tests included), DEMO1's and a route's save/load checksums match the code before the reformat, the level check passes headless and under lavapipe. **CI gate:** the build job runs `dotnet format IsoDoom.sln --verify-no-changes --no-restore --severity warn` after the restore (whitespace, import order and the warning-level rules; about 30 s). |
| 2026-10-08 | **Naming enforced outside ported code (Q4, docs/CODE-QUALITY.md).** The Q3 naming rules (`IDE1006`) are now **warning**, so CI's `dotnet format --verify-no-changes --severity warn` step fails on a misnamed symbol (`dotnet format` reports it but cannot fix it: its rename fixer is not applied in batch). **Rules, rechecked against both guides:** Godot's guide asks `_camelCase` for every private field and has no `s_`/static distinction, while .NET's asks `s_` for static ones: they disagree, so Godot's wins and private `static readonly` fields are `_camelCase` too (`_mapLumps`, not `MapLumps`/`s_mapLumps`). Godot is silent on local constants and local functions, .NET makes both PascalCase, so they are (`const int Width`). Positional record parameters are properties, so PascalCase (Roslyn already treats them so). **Classification:** a file is *ported* when it ports vanilla or Chocolate Doom code under its own names. Ported, exempt (`dotnet_diagnostic.IDE1006.severity = none` by glob at the end of `.editorconfig`): all of `src/Sim` (`mobj_t`, `World.P_*`, `mobjinfo`, …) but its five files of our own code (`Tweaks`, `SimInfo`, `World.AimAssist`, `World.Checksum`, `World.Unported`, which are enforced again); `src/Audio/{MidiFile,Mus2Mid,Opl3,OplMusic,SSound}.cs` (Chocolate Doom's midifile.c, mus2mid.c, opl3.c, i_oplmusic.c; s_sound.c); `src/Game/{FFinale,FFinale.Texts,FWipe,GameFlow,HuStuff,MMenu,StStuff,WiStuff}.cs` (f_finale.c, f_wipe.c, g_game.c/d_main.c, hu_stuff.c, m_menu.c, st_stuff.c, wi_stuff.c). Many other files cite vanilla (Level, Blockmap, Textures, MusSong, WadArchive, LevelScene, SfxPlayer, …) but port its logic under .NET names: they are enforced like the rest. **Mechanism: file globs, plus `[SuppressMessage]` in mixed files.** Globs keep the ported files untouched (no attribute or pragma between vanilla lines, so they stay diffable) and need no upkeep but a line per new ported file. Their consequence, accepted: our own additions inside a ported file (`World.settings`, `World.tweaks`, `SaveGameStart`'s parameters, `GameFlow`'s private `host`, …) are not checked; they follow the vanilla style of the code around them, as before. Splitting the partial classes into ported and new files was not needed: `World`'s new-code parts were already in their own files. **Renames:** 155 declarations in 56 files (74 files with their uses), all with Roslyn's `Renamer.RenameSymbolAsync` over the solution (a throwaway MSBuildWorkspace tool, not committed), no text replacement: private static readonly fields to `_camelCase` (most of them, many in tests), private fields (`ScreenGraphics._wad`, …), local constants to PascalCase, `MMenu.Setup`'s non-vanilla menu routines lose their `M_` (`M_StartBinding` → `StartBinding`, `M_Setup` → `Setup`, …; vanilla's `M_SetupNextMenu` and the rest of `MMenu.cs` keep theirs), `World.unported` → `World.Unported`, RouteGen's `Steer.w` → `Steer.World` and its scripts' `Log` parameter → `log`, a test named `M_Random_…` reworded. Toolchain section: the dev container first; otherwise `global.json`'s SDK, Godot 4.7.2 .NET, and optionally actionlint 1.7.12 + shellcheck, Xvfb/lavapipe and the export templates, Linux x86_64 being what CI and the scripts assume. |
| 2026-10-08 | **Windows build in CI (T8.4).** The Windows x86_64 export (preset `Windows`) is made **on Linux**, in the same job as the Linux one: Godot cross-exports it (its own PE resource editing sets the exe's version info: no Wine or rcedit), and `dotnet publish` takes the win-x64 runtime pack from NuGet; one Godot download and template cache serves both. The build is then tested **on a Windows runner** (`windows-2025`, job `test-windows`) headless: no GPU there, so its checks are the dummy renderer's (no pixel comparison; that stays the Linux export's under lavapipe). Godot's Windows exe is a GUI program whose output a pipe never sees, so the preset exports the **console wrapper always** (`debug/export_console_wrapper=2`, `IsoDoom.console.exe`), not only in debug exports: CI runs everything through it, and players get a way to see the log. Not code-signed. An exported build runs in the exe's folder (Godot changes the working directory to it when the `.pck` is beside it), so CI passes the routes' path absolute; relative paths for players are T8.4b. |
| 2026-10-08 | **macOS build in CI (T8.4a).** The macOS export (preset `macOS`) is made **on Linux** too, in the export job: **universal** (one arm64 + x86_64 executable from the templates' `macos.zip`, and a .NET runtime folder per architecture, `data_IsoDoom_macos_arm64`/`_x86_64`, both published by Godot from NuGet's osx runtimes). A universal (or arm64) export needs `rendering/textures/vram_compression/import_etc2_astc` on in `project.godot`; the project has no VRAM-compressed textures, so it changes nothing else. **Signing: Godot's built-in ad-hoc signature** (`codesign/codesign=1`, with `disable_library_validation`, which ad-hoc signing needs to load the runtime's dylibs): Apple Silicon runs no unsigned arm64 code at all, and there is no Apple identity, so not notarized either (a player opens it the first time with right-click → Open). The `.app` ships in Godot's zip (`IsoDoom-macos-universal`), which keeps the executable bits. **Tested on macOS runners** (`test-macos`: `macos-15`, Apple Silicon, and `macos-15-intel`): unpacked with `ditto`, `lipo` and `codesign --verify`, then the smoke test, both checks and the route play headless, as on Windows (routes' path absolute, T8.4b), with a failure step that turns the logs' error lines into annotations (readable on the run's page without a login). **The music checks' timing:** headless, Godot's dummy audio driver sleeps one mix block (4096 frames) per loop without making up for oversleeping, and on the macOS VMs it drained 0.74 s (arm64) and 0.52 s (Intel) of music per second of wall time, so the viewer and level checks' "rendered at the mix rate" (±0.25 s of the wall clock) failed while the player had its buffer full and no underrun. A short render now fails only when the ring buffer is also under half full (`MusicPlayer.KeepsUp`: the player behind, not the driver); the upper bound, the underruns and the peak are checked as before. Played on an M2 Mac by a person: works. |
| 2026-10-08 | **The music checks in a Debug build (after T8.4a).** The editor runs the checks on a Debug build, whose OPL emulation is about 5 times slower than the export's Release one (the `Opl3Tests` suite: 56 s against 12 s); on a GitHub Linux runner the music thread took 80% of a core there (40% on a 16-core dev machine) and the level check once failed on 28 buffer underruns. A Debug build whose music thread spent over half a core (`MusicPlayer.SlowBuild`; always false in Release) now reports underruns, and a render short of the wall clock, in the viewer and level checks instead of failing on them; the peak, the switches and the upper bound are checked as before, and the exported build's checks (Release) still fail on any underrun or short render. |
| 2026-10-09 | **Releases from tags.** A pushed tag `v*` runs CI as any push, plus a `release` job that needs every other job (lint, build and test, the exports, the Windows and macOS tests), so only a build that passed everything ships. It publishes a GitHub release (`gh release create --generate-notes --verify-tag`, the job alone with `contents: write`) holding the CI artifacts of that run: the Linux and Windows folders zipped as `IsoDoom-TAG-linux-x86_64.zip` / `-windows-x86_64.zip` (the Linux executable's bit set again: artifacts drop it) and Godot's macOS zip as `IsoDoom-TAG-macos-universal.zip`. A tag with a hyphen (`v0.2.0-rc1`) makes a pre-release. **The tag's version is stamped into the builds** by the export job (`tools/stamp-version.sh`, not committed): the whole version (`0.2.0-rc1`, without the v) as `application/config/version`, which the game prints at start (`dev` when empty), and its numeric part (`0.2.0`) as the Windows exe's file and product version and the macOS bundle's versions, since Godot rejects anything but digits and periods there. |

| 2026-10-09 | **Fog of war (T6.13l, SPEC §7.3; not vanilla, presentation only).** Asked for by the user: under the iso camera the whole map and the monsters behind walls showed from the start. **Sight** (`IsoDoom.Render.FogOfWar`, plain C#, `FogOfWarTests`): after each tic (and at a level's start, a load, a script's `place`), from the console player's position, a 360° port of r_bsp.c's `R_RenderBSPNode`/`R_CheckBBox`/`R_AddLine` over 4096 angular columns (0.09° each; a skip list as the occlusion buffer instead of `solidsegs`). Segs facing away are skipped; a seg narrower than a column covers nothing, as vanilla drops one between two screen columns, so a sector seen through a sliver under 0.09° is not seen. A line blocks when it is one-sided or its opening has no height (the lower ceiling at or below the higher floor): p_sight.c `P_CrossSubsector`'s test, not `R_AddLine`'s closed-door test, which lets through a shut door whose floor is above a neighbour's (DOOM1 E1M4's sector 10, E1M9's 20) because vanilla's renderer hides what is behind with its per-column clipping. Heights do not matter otherwise (a window, a step, an open door let sight through). A seg with an open column when reached is seen: its sector (the subsector's) is **visible**, as is the player's own. A dead player keeps its last set. **Cost:** DOOM1 E1M1 from the start about 0.15 ms (11 of 85 sectors visible); worst case, with every shut sector opened, Doom II MAP18 0.12 ms and MAP27 0.25 ms a walk (Debug build), once a tic, not a frame. **`ML_MAPPED`:** the walk sets it in the level's lines, which are the sim's `line_t.flags`, as vanilla's renderer does in r_segs.c `R_StoreWallRange` between tics, on every seen seg's line (also the two-sided lines that draw nothing, which vanilla's renderer skips: the automap draws a line with the same floor and ceiling on both sides only with its cheat). `World.Checksum` leaves line flags out and nothing in the sim reads `ML_MAPPED`, so checksums do not change (`VanillaRouteTests`, `DemoSyncTests`). **Discovered** = a sector with a mapped line on either side: so the saved line flags (`P_ArchiveWorld`) are the whole discovered set, which a load rebuilds (`FogOfWar.Discover`) without a save format change, and the sector across a seen line (a shut door's) is discovered, not visible. **A visible sector's doors** (`DoorLids.FindDoors`, the cutaway's doors: the sectors door specials move) are discovered with it, their lines to it mapped, whether their faces were in sight or not: the user found a door popping in as they walked up to it (DOOM1 E1M1's first), its face out of the walk's sight until then (`FogOfWarTests.AVisibleSectorsDoorsAreDiscovered`). The automap (T7.9) will draw those lines a little before vanilla's would. A level's load and a reborn's reload start with the WAD's flags. The computer map (`pw_allmap`) discovers nothing here: it is the automap's (T7.9). **Drawing:** `sector_fog` (R8, one texel per sector, uploaded when a state changes) and `fog_mode` in the level shader. Every surface takes its own front sector's state (a floor, lid or cap its sector's, a one-sided wall its side's), but a two-sided wall section (upper, lower, masked middle) the more seen of its two sectors', as it is the face of the solid across the line too: a shut door's face on the unseen room's side, which the iso camera often looks at from over the door, showed only the door's lid when it took its own side's room's (the user's report, DOOM1 E1M1's door 4 from the west); likewise the edge of a seen floor above an unseen one. With discovered = mapped lines, a seen line's two sectors are discovered together, so no lit wall hangs over an unseen room; `fog_dim` (R8, 256 entries, `FogOfWar.DimMap`) maps an unseen texel through a fixed COLORMAP row towards grey, whatever its light. **The user chose `hide`** (unseen surfaces collapsed, the void's colour shows) as the default after screenshots under the game camera (DOOM1 E1M1 from the start and beside the computer room's door, E1M2, Doom II MAP01): of the three dim looks (row 20 fully grey; row 24 half grey; row 16 grey with half its pixels dithered to the void) the user took the first for `dim` (`FogDim.Default`, `--level-fog-dim=ROW,GREY[,dither]` to try others), but preferred seeing nothing at all. On a grey level such as E1M2 the dim grey is hard to tell from dark lit rooms. **Things:** the player's own sprite always shows; monsters, barrels, missiles (any `MF_SHOOTABLE`, `MF_COUNTKILL`, `MF_MISSILE` or `MF_CORPSE` thing) and the event marks (puffs, blood, teleport and item fog) only while their sector (the mobj's subsector's) is visible; items, keys and decorations once it is discovered; nothing in an unseen sector. Blob shadows and the fuzz pass follow their billboard. Tracers: the player's own always, with `all` a monster's only from a visible shooter. Sounds and the aim assist (sim) are unaffected. With the fog on, the cursor cutaway (`--level-cutaway-cursor`) no longer shows monsters in closed rooms: they are not drawn (T3.8's note). **Option:** `FogStyle` (`--level-fog=hide\|dim\|off`, default `hide`; setting `gameplay/fog`, Gameplay page: FOG OF WAR; key N), only under the game camera: the overview and free-fly debug cameras draw everything. Overlay line `fog:` (style, visible, discovered and total sectors, the walk's cost); level script `fog` and `seen SECTOR unseen\|discovered\|visible`. **Level check:** on every map after the load the start sector visible, no sector that no line with an opening joins to it visible (REJECT is 3D sight and forbids sectors the 2D walk rightly sees over ledges: Doom II MAP11, MAP17), not every sector discovered, every visible sector with a mapped line and every mapped line's sectors discovered, `sector_fog` equal to the states, the drawn things exactly the rule's (all with the fog off), a shut door seen from 24 units in front discovered and not visible, then opened in the sim visible with the sector across it, and the reborn's fog as fresh as the load's; the save check's loaded game has the saved discovered set. With a real renderer every floor top-down with `dim` (each unseen pixel the dim mapping's colour) and `hide` (the background), and an imp in sight drawn while one in the discovered start room out of sight is not (drawn with the fog off). **Not done:** a fade-in when a sector is discovered; per-thing sight (a monster behind a pillar in a visible sector shows: sight is by sector). |

A zip holding `DOOM1.WAD` directly is taken too; one level of nesting only. **Verification:** the WAD must be 4,196,020 bytes with MD5 `f0cefca49926d00903cf57551d901abe` (the community's identifier) and SHA-256 `1d7d43be501e67d927e415e0b8f3e29c3bf33075e859721816f652a526cac771` (the tamper check); downloads over 8 MB are cut off. **Where:** `user://wads/DOOM1.WAD` (always writable; the game folder may not be: Program Files, a macOS `.app`), written as `.part` then moved, and saved as the configured IWAD (`[wad] iwad`), so the next start finds it; if the setting is lost, the download button reuses a copy there that still verifies instead of fetching it again. `user://wads` is not added to the search folders (it would make the shareware beat a Steam install found later). **Code:** `IsoDoom.Wad.SharewareDownload` (plain C#, `HttpClient`, no new dependency; unit-tested offline with a faked handler and installer-shaped zips, and against the real `DOOM1.WAD`), `WadLocator.DownloadShareware` (one shared client, 60 s timeout, `User-Agent: IsoDoom/VERSION`). **UI:** the IWAD menu's none-found case (T7.2) gets a focused first button, "Download the shareware episode", with the progress and mirror under the buttons; meanwhile only Quit is live; a failure names each mirror's error and leaves the menu as it was; success chooses it as the picker does. **`--download-shareware`** does the same before the game starts (any display, headless too) and quits with 1 on failure. Checked: live, headless (`--download-shareware --level E1M1`), and with the Linux export under Xvfb in an empty `HOME` (menu, progress from gamers.org, title loop). The WAD viewer's picker (a debug tool) does not offer it. |

| 2026-10-09 | **Instant stop (T6.13o, SPEC §6.3).** Requested by the user: the player slid on after a move key was let go (vanilla's friction keeps 0.90625 of the momentum a tic, so a running player slides about 150 units). **Tweak `Tweaks.InstantStop`** (on in `TopDown`, off in `Vanilla`, so routes, demos and `--level-tweaks=vanilla` are unchanged): `World.P_InstantStop`, called by `P_MovePlayer` on the ground before the thrust, keeps only the part of the momentum along the new input's direction (north/east with `AbsoluteMovement`, else along and right of the facing), none against it or without input, and a stopped player leaves its walking frames as the friction stop does. **Only when the input changes** (`player_t.lastforwardmove`/`lastsidemove`, the last input it moved with on the ground, not vanilla's): projecting every tic would also eat the momentum along a wall the player slides on while pushing diagonally into it (about 6 times slower slides) and every knockback, so under unchanged input, and after a stop, vanilla's friction runs as before; input changed in the air applies on landing. Starting to move still accelerates as vanilla's (about 20 tics to full speed). A stick's direction changes often, so with a pad momentum off the stick's new direction is dropped each such tic. Saved (the tweak and both fields: save format version 2, older saves refused). |
| 2026-10-11 | **Switch markers (T6.13p, SPEC §7.2; not vanilla, presentation only).** Requested by the user: the game camera looks north-west (T3.3), so a switch on a wall facing north or west is never drawn (its face is culled, and it has no wall cap): DOOM1 has 18 such switch sides, among them the exits of E1M2 (line 873), E1M3 (982), E1M6 (627) and E1M7 (810) and E1M3's secret exit (785). Rejected: rotating the camera (§7.1), drawing the wall's back face (it would hide the room, and the cutaway would remove it as the player comes near). **Decision:** a decal on the floor in front of each switch side, `IsoDoom.Render.SwitchMarkers` (plain C#, `SwitchMarkersTests`), drawn by the level mesh (vertex kind 9, in its sector's chunk). **Which sides:** every drawn wall section (upper, lower or middle) whose texture is in the game's switch pairs (p_switch.c `alphSwitchList`, `Switches.For`), with or without a special (it looks like a switch either way); a side with several takes the first of upper, middle, lower (`P_ChangeSwitchTexture`'s order). All are built; the shader shows a marker **unless its wall faces the camera** (its normal's dot product with the flattened direction towards the camera above 0.001: facing away, i.e. less than 90° off the view direction, or edge-on, or seen straight down), so exactly the switches whose quad is culled get one, under every camera (DOOM1: 45 switch sides, 18 shown from the game camera; the task's 60° count was 17, E1M3 line 360 lies between 60° and 90°), and while the switch's section has a height (`WallSection.Span`, as the walls': a lift's switch below the floor shows none). **What it shows:** the switch itself: the rows where the pair's two textures differ (`SwitchMarkers.Band`: 10–22 rows for most of DOOM1's and Doom II's, SW1SKULL 65, SW1DIRT all 128), grown evenly to at least 16 or cut evenly to at most 32 (the middle rows), one texel a unit as on the wall; the whole side's width, its columns tiled as on the wall (`textureoffset` and scroll), **mirrored** about the side's middle and the rows going up away from the wall (the wall laid back onto the floor, read from the camera's side as a player in front of it reads it). Its texture is the side's current one (`side_textures`), so a used switch shows its other texture and a repeatable one changes back. Clipped to its sector's floor (the subsector polygons), so it never covers another floor or the void (a ledge shallower than the switch gets a shallower marker). **Drawn** 1 unit above the floor (`SwitchMarkers.Lift`), following it as it moves, lit as a floor of the sector (`v_kind` 3: a wall's texel, a floor's light), under the fog of war as its sector, cut by the cutaway as a floor. No fallback look (a strip in the texture's average colour) was needed: in DOOM1 the panels read as switches flat (E1M3's exit: a grey panel with its red light). **Option:** `SwitchMarkerMode`, `--level-switch-markers=on\|off`, setting `gameplay/switch_markers` (SWITCH MARKERS), key R, overlay line when off; default on. **Menu:** the Gameplay page had grown to 18 rows, past the screen's bottom (rows every 10 from y 36, the hint at 188); its wall options (door lids, wall tops, upper walls, masked walls behind) moved with the switch markers to a new **Walls** page (More Options → Walls…, `menu walls`), their keys unchanged; `SettingsTests` now checks every text page fits above its hint. **Checked:** `SwitchMarkersTests` (the band's limits; the synthetic E1M2's five SW1BRCOM sides, lines 0 and 85 shown from the game camera, 1, 27 and 84 not; the mirrored columns and rows; a shallow ledge clipping; every DOOM1 switch side with a marker on its sector's floor, and its exits facing away shown); `--level-check` on every map (every switch side has a marker, shown exactly when its wall faces away from the game camera; the chunk vertices) and with a real renderer E1M3 line 982's marker straight down, every pixel against SW1COMP and, after the switch changes as a use does, SW2COMP (1178 marker pixels each), and from the game camera's angle a marker facing away drawn and one facing the camera not (DOOM1 E1M1 line 330, SW1STRTN; the synthetic E1M1's L6, which draws `SW1BRCOM` now so that CI's render check views a marker; it fails with the rows one off). The sim is untouched (`Sim/` unchanged; the checksums with it). |
| 2026-10-11 | **Item beams (T6.13r, SPEC §7.5; not vanilla, presentation only).** Requested by the user: under the game camera (pitch 55°) a wall of height h hides the floor up to h / tan 55° ≈ 0.7 h behind it, so a pickup just behind a wall seen from the camera's side is hidden whole while a vanilla player in the room sees it (DOOM1 E1M1: the health bonus at (1312, −3520) behind the start room's pillar and the nukage pool's wall); the cutaway opens walls near the player only. Isowulf draws a line from the item into the sky. **Rejected:** an x-ray silhouette of the item through the wall (it fights the "walls hide" look the fog and the cutaway work for, and in the iso view it is hard to tell which room a silhouette is in; a beam rises above the walls, so the room is clear); beams on every item, hidden or not (clutter in rooms full of pickups); a bigger cutaway radius (it removes walls far from the player and still misses items across the map). **Decision:** `IsoDoom.Render.ItemBeams` (a MultiMesh, one instance per beam) and `shaders/item_beam.gdshader`. **Which things:** mobjs with `MF_SPECIAL` (info.c's 36 pickup types, dropped ones too), among those drawn, so only where the fog lets them be drawn (T6.13l: an item once its sector is discovered; a beam never reveals an undiscovered sector), under the game camera only (not the overview or free-fly). **Kinds and colours** (`IsoDoom.Render.ItemKinds`, plain C#, by sprite as p_inter.c's `P_TouchSpecialThing`): keys and skull keys their colour (blue 0.25/0.5/1, yellow 1/0.9/0.1, red 1/0.1/0.1), weapons orange (1/0.55/0), power-ups and the big ones (spheres, invulnerability, blur, suit, visor, map, berserk, backpack) magenta (0.9/0.35/1), armor and armor bonuses green (0.25/1/0.3), health pink (1/0.45/0.7), ammo tan (0.75/0.6/0.35); sRGB, unshaded, full bright, none near the tracers' colours. **Hidden** (decided by the GPU, since the cutaway, lids, wall caps, upper walls and fog all change what hides what): the beam's vertex shader puts up to 8 × 8 of the item patch's opaque texels where the sprite shader draws them (the tilt, the move back onto the upright plane with upright tilt depth, the wall pull; rows below the origin at the foot's height) and reads the depth texture at each; the beam shows only when something is more than 1 unit in front of every one. The depth texture holds what the opaque pass drew: the walls, floors, masked middles, door lids, wall caps and switch markers (all the level's opaque material, `depth_draw_opaque`) and, unless the upright hiding is on, the opaque thing sprites, the item's own among them: so a decoration standing in front of an item and covering it whole also beams it. **Actors do not** (user request, 2026-10-11: on DOOM1 E1M3 a zombie walking over an armor bonus in plain sight flashed its beam): `ItemBeams.Set` gives each beam the 4 actors (monsters, barrels, missiles, the player) nearest its item within 160 units, and a texel whose occluder lies on one's billboard as the sprite shader draws it (its plane within 2 units, upright or tilted with the tilt depth, moved by the wall pull; or its rows below the origin on the floor plane) counts as seen when the actor stands on the item (their radii + 8 apart: what hides the item there hides the actor too) and does not count when it stands farther in front (an item behind a wall keeps its beam while a monster walks past); an item with no texel counted is not hidden. What is behind an actor is not in the depth texture, so this is the rule: rejected, drawing the actors after the depth copy (in the transparent pass), which would be exact but loses the fuzz over them (a spectre in front of an imp). Known edge: an actor whose wall pull draws it in front of the wall hiding an item it stands on (under a lintel) turns the beam off while it stands there. A texel off screen counts as seen (no beam for an item off screen whose beam would poke into view), as does one under the status bar (the 3D view runs under it). With the upright hiding (T3.6a) the sprite shader drops an item whole when its upright billboard is hidden whole, so that counts as hidden too. A partly hidden item gets no beam (it shows); no threshold was needed. A wall the cutaway cuts writes no depth, so a cut-open item shows and has no beam; its dithered style leaves holes, so it mostly does too. **Look:** a ribbon facing the camera (turned about the vertical, as the billboards), 2.5 units wide (the plan's 1.5 drew 2 pixels at the default zoom), 256 units high (the plan's 160 showed only its faded top above E1M1's wall: the wall hides the beam up to h + 1.43 d with the item d behind it), opaque to two thirds of its height and fading out above; depth-tested and not writing depth, drawn in the transparent pass (it reads the depth texture), so the wall that hides the item hides the beam's foot and it rises from behind its top (and behind any pillar in front of it). Static (no pulse); a diamond at the top was tried and dropped. **Option:** `ItemBeamMode`, `--level-item-beams=off\|keys\|all` (`keys`: keys, weapons and power-ups only, what a player would go back for), setting `gameplay/item_beams` on the Sprites page (ITEM BEAMS; the Gameplay page is full, T6.13p), key F, overlay line when not `all`; default `all` (beams only show for hidden items, so E1M1's armor and zigzag rooms show none). Script `beams` lists the beamed items. **Checked:** `ItemKindsTests`; `--level-check` on every map headless (the beamed items are exactly the drawn pickups the mode beams with the fog hide, none undiscovered, and with it off; after the fog check's door opens, the items across it) and with a renderer (each pickup alone from the game camera: one showing a quarter of its texels has no beam pixel; one hidden whole has beam pixels of its kind's colour over what is behind it, none where it shows once cut open and some above; cut open by the cutaway, no beam; the option off, nothing changes; an item in an undiscovered sector under `hide`, no beam; a map whose pickups give no case, such as the synthetic E1M1, which has none, gets spawned keycards behind its tallest walls facing the camera and at its start, removed afterwards). **Cost** under lavapipe at 1280×800 on E1M1: about 1.3–1.8 ms a frame (16.7 → 18.0–18.5 ms median at the start, 11 beamed; +1.2–1.5 ms at zoom 1600 with the fog off, 52 beamed), mostly the depth texture's copy, as the upright hiding's. The sim is untouched (`Sim/` unchanged; the checksums with it). |
| 2026-10-12 | **Height cues (T6.13s, SPEC §7.2; not vanilla, presentation only).** Requested by the user after a player's comment that the architecture "folds in on itself" so it is hard to tell what is above or below. Under the fixed camera (T3.3, north-west at 55°) a raised floor seen from above looks like any other floor, and the lower wall below a ledge's edge is culled whenever it faces away (north or west): DOOM1 has 18–38 such ledges a map (E1M8's 344-unit pit among them), whose drop nothing shows. Of the options weighed (marking who the player can hit and who can hit it, which answers the fight question better and stays open; shadows and drop lines; arrows at the screen's edge) the user picked the level geometry's own readability. **Decision:** both looks, behind one option, for the user to compare. **Ledge edges:** `IsoDoom.Render.LedgeEdges` (plain C#): each side of every two-sided line with different sectors gets a strip 3 units wide on its sector's floor along the line, clipped to that sector's subsector polygons (so it never leaves its floor), drawn by the level mesh (vertex kind 10, in its sector's chunk) 0.5 units above the floor (under the switch markers' 1) while that floor is more than **24 units** above the floor across the line (p_map.c's step height: a drop the player cannot step back up; steps and stairs get none). The heights are the shader's (`sector_data`), so a lift shows its edge while up and loses it at the bottom. **Look:** PLAYPAL index 80 (light grey) with its innermost unit index 0 (a dark rim, so it reads on bright floors too), through the plane light tables at the floor's light number + 4 and at least 10 (so it fades with distance as floors do but stays above its floor, and shows in dark rooms), under the fog as its sector, cut by the cutaway as a floor, kept on doors with them. Drawn on the high side only (the low side's foot is its wall, seen whenever it faces the camera). **Height shading:** every surface lit as a floor (floors, their cutaway caps, lids, wall caps, switch markers, edges) gains a light level per **24 units** it is above the player's feet (`height_reference`, its mobj's interpolated z, under any camera) and loses one per 24 below, rounded to nearest, at most **5** either way, but darkened no lower than light level **8** of 16 (`LedgeEdges.ShadeMinLight`, the shader's `shade_light`; a surface already below it is not darkened): one level is 4 of COLORMAP's 32 rows, so 5 down plus the light diminishing took an ordinary room (light 128–160) under a drop to black (E1M8's lower room from its start); walls are not shaded (they span heights). Relative to the player rather than absolute, since the question is "above or below me": it changes as the player climbs. **Option:** `HeightCueMode` (a bit mask in the shader's `height_cues`: 1 edges, 2 shade), `--level-height-cues=edges\|shade\|both\|off`, setting `gameplay/height_cues` on the Walls page (HEIGHT CUES), key Z (cycles shade, edges, both, off), overlay line when not `shade`; default `shade`: the user, having looked, kept the shading and not the edges (2026-10-12; `edges` was the default until then). The sim is untouched (`Sim/` unchanged; the checksums with it). |
| 2026-10-12 | **Sight dim (T6.13t, SPEC §7.5; not vanilla, presentation only).** From the height-readability discussion (`docs/height-readability-options.md`, a player's "difficult to tell what is above you, below you, or merely waiting for you with a fireball"). In vanilla's first-person view "if you see it, it sees you" holds nearly by construction: a monster attacks only once `P_CheckSight` gives it a line of sight from its eyes. The iso camera floats above the player and sees over walls, ledges and pillars that block that line, so monsters that cannot attack look like those that can. **Rule:** vanilla's own: a drawn mobj that is not the console player's, alive (`health > 0`), `MF_SHOOTABLE` and a chaser (`info.seestate` not `S_NULL`: monsters and lost souls; not barrels, corpses, items, missiles) is dimmed while `World.P_CheckSight(monster, player)` is false: monster as `t1`, the direction that governs its attacks (`A_Look`, `A_Chase`, `P_CheckMissileRange`; sight is not symmetric, each end's eye differs). Under the game camera only; with `--level-fog-things=sight` monsters out of sector sight are still not drawn, and the rest are dimmed by this per-thing 3D rule, so a monster behind a pillar in a visible sector or on a ledge whose lip hides it is caught too (T6.13l's "per-thing sight"). Not touched: sounds, the aim assist, the item beams, the tracers. **When:** once per tic, after the fog's sight walk (`LevelScene.SeeSight`, also at level start, load and the script's `place`), never per frame; a dead player keeps the last result. **Why calling the sim's `P_CheckSight` between tics is safe:** it writes `validcount` (incremented first, then only compared for equality with the lines' and sectors' copies, so an extra increment changes no outcome of any later search), `sightcounts` (statistics no code reads) and `sightzstart`/`topslope`/`bottomslope`/`strace`/`t2x`/`t2y` (set afresh at the start of every check); none is in `World.Checksum` or the save. The route, demo-sync and save tests are unchanged, and the level check finds the checksum unchanged across the checks. Under lockstep co-op each peer's `validcount` would drift apart by its own checks, harmless for the same reason. **Cost** (headless Debug build, every monster awake, fog off so all are checked): DOOM1 E1M9 at skill 4, 103 monsters, 0.10–0.17 ms a tic; Doom II MAP27, 170, 0.34–0.37 ms; MAP29, 107, 0.33–0.51 ms; REJECT answers many. Well under §9's 2 ms, so no culling to the screen's bounds. **Fade** (`IsoDoom.Render.SightDim`, plain C#, keyed by the mobj's reference, cleared at each load): into sight undimmed the same tic (a threat must show at once); out of sight a 4-tic grace (walking past a doorframe or pillar does not blink), then 2 colormap rows a tic up to the amount; a monster first drawn (spawned, or let through by the fog) starts at its target; mobjs gone drop out. **Look:** the sprite shader adds the rows to the colormap row it draws through (the light from the sector, distance and `extralight`, or 0 for full-bright frames): `max(row, min(row + dim, 29))`, so a dimmed monster in a dark room stays faintly visible instead of black. Full-bright frames dim too (an imp out of sight throwing a fireball reads as out of sight); a fixed colormap (invulnerability, the visor) still wins; the outline goes through the same row; the fuzz pass is untouched (already dark). The rows ride in the instance's flags above the existing five bits (`flags | dim << 5`; the other bits' tests use `&`). Because COLORMAP's rows darken unevenly, 10 rows is a moderate step in a bright room and close to black in a dark one (DOOM1 E1M2's maze at light 112). **Option:** `--level-sight-dim=ROWS|off` (1–31; `off` is vanilla), `sprites/sight_dim` (Sprites page, SIGHT DIM: off, 6, 10, 14; the page has room), key Y (6, 10, 14, off; the menus take their y/n first), default **6**: the user's pick after seeing 6, 10 and 14 (10 and 14 take monsters in dark rooms nearly to black). **Rejected:** transparency (alpha-sorted billboards and off-palette colours; a dither reads as the cutaway's or the fuzz's); hiding (that is `--level-fog-things=sight`, per sector); a dim keyed to "can the player hit it" (`P_AimLineAttack` changes `linetarget` and the shot's slopes, which the sim reads; option 1 of the options doc, still open): a monster in sight that the player's autoaim cannot reach stays lit by this rule, which is right, since it can shoot. |

The T6.8 option was all or nothing, and off also dropped the power-up colormaps. **Decision:** `gameplay/palette_effects` / `--level-palette-effects` becomes `screen\|player\|off` (`on` reads as `screen`: a file from before needs no migration, and since only non-default values are saved it is dropped from the file), and a new `gameplay/flash_strength` / `--level-flash-strength` 10–100 % (the menu steps by 10; the argument takes any whole percentage), next to it on the Gameplay page. At 100 the row is the flash palette's own, vanilla's pixel for pixel. The fuzz's `nearest_index` searches the row the screen is drawn in (the blend too): it matches the screen's colours back to their indices, so it darkens the blended screen as vanilla's darkens the flashed one. The HUD, the pause graphic and the menus (drawn on the CPU, T6.11) take the same 768 bytes; the void is lerped from its own colour to the flash's colour 0 by the strength; the blob shadows take the row's colour 0. **Player style:** the level, its sprites, the HUD, the menus and the void stay on palette 0; the player's own sprite (`ThingSprites.FlagOwn`, as the player light) reads a second uniform, `own_palette_index`, the flash's row at the strength (a fuzzed pixel of it shows the screen behind it, in the screen's row); and a glow (`IsoDoom.Render.FlashGlow`, `shaders/flash_glow.gdshader`): a disc of radius 40 units (a little wider than the aim marker's ring) half a unit above the floor at the interpolated player, alpha-blended, depth-tested and not writing depth (the player's sprite covers its far half), its alpha at most 0.6 at the centre falling smoothly (smoothstep) to 0 at the rim, times the flash's level and the strength. **Its colour** comes from PLAYPAL, so a PWAD's palettes change it as they change vanilla's flashes: st_stuff.c's palettes are palette 0 moved a fraction f towards a colour T, so per channel `pn = (1 − f) p0 + f T`; a least-squares line over the 256 colours gives the slope 1 − f and the intercept f T (DOOM1: red (255, 0, 0), gold about (215, 186, 69), the suit's green (0, 255, 0) clamped); a palette no different from palette 0 (f < 0.02) gives no glow. **Its level:** red palettes 1–8 at n/8, gold 9–12 at (n − 8)/4 (vanilla's gold palettes are weak, 12.5–50 %, and a pickup's must still show), the suit's 13 at 0.25. Under the game camera only (or the check's `FlashOnPlayerEverywhere`); the overview and free-fly cameras fall back to `screen` at the strength, so debugging still shows the flash. The glow shows with the player's sprite (not when things are hidden). **Power-up colormaps** (`fixedcolormap`: invulnerability, the visor) stay with both `screen` and `player`, unscaled: they last the power and say it is active (the user's decision); `off` drops them, the photosensitivity switch. **No debug key** (the few keys left are kept for debugging; the menu and the arguments have it); the overlay shows `palette flashes: STYLE N%` unless `screen` at 100. **Checked:** `PaletteFlashTests`; `SettingsTests`; `--level-check` on every map headless (each style at 100 and 50 % after a damage and a pickup flash and with invulnerability: the palette, strength and flag, the level's and the player's rows, the blend row's bytes, the colormap override, the void, the shadows, the glow's visibility, colour and alpha; the player style without the game camera and with the player's sprite hidden), the settings (both changed, through a file and a reset; `on` in a file read as `screen`, not kept), and with a renderer the floors, a thing (also as the player's own sprite) and the fuzz in blended and player-only palettes, every pixel against the CPU, and the glow straight down at the player's feet (all its pixels within its radius, the centre redder at half and more at full strength). The T6.8 checks at 100 % pass unchanged. |
