| 2026-10-13 | **Vanilla's screens at 4:3 (T7.11, SPEC §7.6).** Vanilla drew a 320×200 screen that a 4:3 CRT showed with pixels 1.2 times taller than wide (`(4/3) / (320/200)`), and id's art was drawn for it; square pixels squash it (round things look flat). **Option** `hud/aspect` (More Options → HUD, ASPECT; `--level-hud-aspect=4:3\|square`), presentation like `hud/scale`, not a sim tweak: `4:3`, **the default** (the right look), or `square`, the old layout pixel for pixel (the vanilla comparisons, and players who prefer it). **Two layouts** (`ScreenLayout`): the HUD (the status bar, the fullscreen HUD, the message line, the automap's title) only has to fit across, so it keeps the whole scale k across (the largest at which 320×200 fits) and takes 1.2 k down: the bar as wide as before and 1.2 times taller (1280×154 at 1280×800, at most 19.2% of the window; at the full screens' scale it would be 1067 wide, pillarboxed beside the level for nothing). The full screens and the menus must fit whole at 320×240's proportions: k = min(W / 320, H / 240), **not rounded down** (1280×800 would waste a quarter of the window at k = 3), centred; the menus over the level are then a little smaller than the bar (3.33 against 4 at 1280×800), as vanilla's never lined up with the bar's pixels either. A fixed `hud/scale` N is the scale across, 1.2 N down. Every rectangle's edges round to whole pixels and each view maps through its own rectangle, so the drawn pixels and the mouse agree. **Sharp bilinear** (`SharpBilinear`, Chocolate Doom's fix): at 4.8 rows per pixel nearest filtering makes rows uneven (5, 5, 5, 5, 4: the fonts' strokes vary); the shader samples each texel's core exactly and blends only the pixel on a block's edge, as a whole-number nearest upscale filtered linearly to size; premultiplied alpha (`HudScreen.ToRgba`'s see-through pixels are 0, 0, 0, 0), so no dark fringe where the level shows beside the bar and the menus; at a whole scale it is nearest exactly, and the views then use nearest without the shader. The CPU mirrors it for the wipe's start frame and the level check. **Automap:** low detail's frame pixels are the HUD's, 1.2 times taller, so its lines take vanilla's vertical stretch, as on a CRT and as the bar under it; high detail stays one window pixel per frame pixel, true to the map. **The level's 3D view is left alone:** it is our own view in map units, not vanilla's 320×200 one, and walls and sprites share its scale (vanilla's 3D view was stretched 1.2 times too, walls and sprites together, so their proportions already match ours); stretching sprites alone would make them taller than their walls and hit boxes. Nor the IWAD menu (Godot controls) or the free-fly vanilla view (`compare.sh`, square on purpose). **Comparisons:** the level check runs square but for its own 4:3 section (as its wipes: the option is ignored under the check), and `hud.sh`, `wi.sh`, `fi.sh` and `wipe.sh` pass `--level-hud-aspect=square`, so they stay pixel for pixel against vanilla's 320×200 × 4. |

| 2026-10-14 | **Level resources disposed on unload (fix, `docs/issues/level-resource-disposal`; presentation only).** A level's Godot resources were left to the .NET GC, which cannot see their native and GPU memory: dead levels' meshes and textures stayed (about 170 MB more under lavapipe before RSS levelled off), and thousands of wrappers still waiting for finalization at exit could crash Godot's C# teardown (`godot-shutdown-leak`). **Decision:** they are disposed explicitly: `LevelMesh` is `IDisposable` (its chunk meshes, materials, textures and images; twice is safe), `ThingSprites.ReleaseResources()` disposes its multimeshes and meshes, and `LevelScene.UnloadLevel` calls both before freeing the nodes; the `MusicPlayer`'s `AudioStreamGenerator` is disposed in `StopThread()`, the player never playing again after it; temporary `Image`s made only to fill a texture are disposed at once. `Dispose()` on a RefCounted wrapper only drops the C# reference, so a node still drawing with one (the player sprite keeps the last level's material until the next `Bind`) keeps it alive; C# code must not touch an old level's `LevelMesh` after a load (`ObjectDisposedException`): the level check re-reads `_scene.Mesh` after its reborn. |
| 2026-10-14 | **Mod formats after v1 (SPEC §5.3; a plan, nothing built).** DeHackEd, UMAPINFO, Boom (with extended nodes and BEX) and MBF21 are worth supporting after v1, in that order, each behind a compatibility level so the vanilla sim stays exact; ID24 is to watch; Hexen format, UDMF and ZDoom are never supported and get a clear refusal. Why: a survey of four classic vanilla megawads (Scythe, Alien Vendetta, D2TWID, Memento Mori) found them loading, but two ship `.deh` patches that are ignored, and the popular modern PWADs target Boom or MBF21. |
| 2026-10-14 | **Relative paths in exported builds (T8.4b).** Godot's export templates change the working directory at the top of `Main::setup`, before any C# runs, to the executable's folder (on macOS the bundle's `Resources`), on every platform and whether or not the `.pck` is embedded (`binary_format/embed_pck` was tried: it changes nothing; the old directory is kept only to look for a `.pck` there). So relative path arguments resolve against the **launch directory**, `WadLocator.LaunchDirectory`: in an export the shell's `$PWD` when it is set, absolute and exists (Linux, macOS from a terminal), else the process's working directory; in the editor always the process's, which is the shell's. `WadLocator.ResolveUserPath` applies it to `--savedir`, `--settings`, `--level-screenshot`, `--viewer-screenshots` and the level script's `route FILE` and `shot FILE.png`; `-iwad`, `-file` and the IWAD search's working-directory step already took it. **Windows keeps the game's folder:** nothing of the shell's directory survives there (cmd and PowerShell set no `PWD`, Git Bash's is a `/c/…` path), and a launcher or reading the parent process's directory was judged not worth it for arguments players rarely type; there, give absolute paths (CI's Windows route play does). |

| 2026-10-15 | **T10.7 review: saves and the game version.** A save plays as the executable it was saved as (`World.gameversion`, in the save's state), while the run's flow (title loop, menus, `HU_Start`'s titles, the intermission, the finale) plays as `-gameversion`'s, or else the IWAD's. So `G_DoLoadGame` refuses a save made as another executable than the run's, with its own message ("this savegame is for another game version and can't be loaded.", `SaveGameFile.OTHERVERSION`: not another game's, which would mislead a player who only changed `-gameversion`), right after the game mode check and before the trial load, and the game goes on unchanged. This is vanilla's refusal of another version's save (`G_DoLoadGame`'s version string), with a message where vanilla returns silently. The save format is unchanged (`SaveGameFile.VERSION` 4); the version is read from the state's start (`SaveGameFile.ReadGameVersion`). A saved version that `GameVersions.Emulated` excludes (v1.2, v1.5, Hacx, Chex Quest, which `-gameversion` refuses too) makes the save damaged. |
| 2026-10-15 | **T10.7 review: the version's default.** A world built without an explicit `gameversion` resolves it as `GameVersions.Default(gamemode, gamemission)`, with the mission carried in `SpawnSettings` (default `none`: the game mode's default alone). The mission is not saved: a save stores the version it resolved to, so the save format is unchanged. A route plays as its `gameversion` header, else its `iwad` header's default (`final` for `tnt` and `plutonia`, `1.9` for `doom1`, `doom2`, `synthetic` and `testmap`), through `RouteFile.ImpliedGameVersion`. The tests and the level script's `route` share it, and the script fails when the running game's version differs. So a DOOM1 route played on a registered or Ultimate IWAD (default `ultimate`) needs `-gameversion 1.9`. `HU_Start` and `HU_Title` take the version with no default. |

A target level with the shooter gets a flight of 0, which `A_SpawnFly` counts past, so the cube flies on, as vanilla's does. | 2026-10-15 | **Regression checksums (T8.6).** Every route of `tests/IsoDoom.Tests/Sim/Routes` and the IWADs' 15 demos (DOOM1's, Doom II's as v1.9 and as `final`, TNT's, Plutonia's) play against committed expected values in `Routes/checksums.txt` (`RouteChecksumTests`, `RouteChecksums.cs`). **The gap it closes:** the synthetic IWAD's and the test maps' routes already ran in CI against their committed vanilla dumps, but those dumps hold only what dump.c writes (the player, sectors, textures, a 32-bit hash of the mobjs' type/state/position/flags/health, …), and the id IWADs' routes and demos (131 routes, 15 demos) need vanilla's dumps, which are WAD-derived, live in `~/.cache` and never reach CI or another checkout. **The format:** per tic, `World.Checksum()` (64 bits: every mobj's momentum, AI fields and target too) and the tic's dump line (`VanillaRoute.Line`, the HUD without its pixels, so a synthetic route's value does not depend on DOOM1.WAD's graphics being there) are fed into one 64-bit FNV-1a chain; the chain is written folded to 32 bits (8 hex digits) after every 35th tic and after the last: `NAME TICS HASH…`, one line per route, sorted (38 KB for 170 entries; a per-tic list would be about 1.2 MB). The chain makes any differing tic change every later hash, so the first differing hash names the second it happened in; the vanilla dump, where there is one, then names the tic. `iwad NAME MD5` lines record the IWAD the id IWADs' entries were made on: a route on another version (Doom II v1.666 against v1.9) skips rather than fails. **Not WAD content:** the file holds only hashes of sim state and tic counts (the routes' own lengths); no text, lump data or pixels, so it is committed as the routes are, unlike the vanilla dumps (whose text, e.g. the texture names, is WAD-derived). **Regenerating:** `ISODOOM_UPDATE_CHECKSUMS=1 dotnet test --filter "FullyQualifiedName~RouteChecksumTests"` rewrites the entries of the routes that ran under a lock (the tests run in parallel) and keeps the rest, so a contributor without the WADs rewrites the synthetic ones only. `EveryRouteHasCommittedChecksums` (no WAD) fails a route without an entry and an entry without a route or demo. **Proof:** `FRICTION` 0xe800 → 0xe801 fails 23 of the 24 synthetic and test-map routes without any WAD and 127 of the 170 entries with every IWAD; `BASETHRESHOLD` 100 → 99, which `VanillaRouteTests` misses (the dumps hold no `threshold`), fails 4 without any WAD (`synthetic-monsters`, `testmap-death`, `testmap-missiles`, `testmap-weapons`). `VanillaRoute.Check`'s tic loop moved to `VanillaRoute.Play`, which both use. |
| 2026-10-15 | **Allocations and the JIT (T8.3, the part without a GPU or a Deck).** **No garbage per tic or frame:** the sim allocates only the mobjs it spawns, as vanilla's `Z_Malloc` (36–106 bytes a tic, `TicAllocationTests`, budget 192); the static tables of more than a byte an entry (`finesine`, `finetangent`, `tantoangle`, `Ticcmds.angleturn`, the OPL3's `logsinrom`/`exprom`) are arrays behind their span properties, because a span over static data of `int`/`ushort` allocates at every read in a Debug build (the editor's: about 700 KB a frame from the music thread, 150 collections in 700 tics). The presentation makes nothing per frame on its usual paths: `StringName`s made once (`GameInput`'s actions, `Render.StringNames` for shader parameters and buses), the user arguments read once, reused buffers and lists, no closures or interface enumerators in the per-frame loops, `World.Mobjs()` a struct enumerator. The F3 overlay's text is rebuilt ten times a second, and the settings are compared with the saved ones four times a second and when the scene leaves (the volumes and the mouse sensitivity apply every frame). Measured (headless, E1M9 skill 4, every monster hunting, 700 live tics): release export 4 → 0 collections, worst tic 2.0–3.2 → 0.7–1.0 ms, mean 0.12–0.13 ms, p95 0.23–0.24 ms; editor 154 → 1. **JIT warm-up:** the JIT compiled each sim path at its first call, so the first tics that took one (tic 19 of E1M9 with monsters woken: the first attacks) took 5–11 ms. `JitWarmup` compiles every non-generic method of Sim, Map and Wad and of the per-tic presentation types (`RuntimeHelpers.PrepareMethod`) on a background thread at start: about 2100 methods in 0.2–0.3 s, running none of them, so no state, random index, sound or checksum can change, unlike a throwaway world's tics. The first 105 tics' worst: export 4.9–8.0 → 0.7–1.1 ms, editor 7.6–11.0 → 0.8–2.0 ms; loads unchanged (all within §9: Doom II's MAP01, the first, 544 ms headless on a busy machine, DOOM1 240 ms). ReadyToRun (`PublishReadyToRun`, honoured by Godot's .NET export) loads E1M9 in 100 ms instead of 158 but its Linux export crashed in 2 of 4 Doom II level checks: not adopted (T8.3a). TieredPGO and tiered compilation stay the runtime's defaults. The fps on integrated graphics and the Deck stay T8.3's and T8.1's, for people. |
| 2026-10-15 | **Timing tests run alone (T8.6a).** The tests that assert on wall time against SPEC §9's budgets (`TicBudgetTests`, `FogOfWarCostTests`, `FinalDoomLoadTests`) are in one xunit collection, `Timing` (`TimingGroup.Name`), defined with `DisableParallelization = true`: xunit v3 runs it on its own after every parallel collection. **Why:** xunit runs test classes in parallel, so the rest of the suite competed for the cores while they measured and they failed at random (four times in one day, a different test each time), yet passed alone; their numbers are well inside the budgets (MAP27's tic p95 0.47 ms of 2 ms). The budgets stay SPEC §9's; loosening them would hide a real regression. Only the timed tests move (split out of `FogOfWarTests` and `FinalDoomTests`), so the serial phase is about 1 s. | 2026-10-15 | **ReadyToRun exports (T8.3a; §9).** `PublishReadyToRun` is on for the `ExportRelease` configuration (`IsoDoom.csproj`), so the Linux, Windows and macOS exports ship precompiled assemblies (`IsoDoom.dll` 1.47 → 4.1 MB); the editor and Debug builds still JIT, so `JitWarmup` stays (20 ms of work left in an export). On DOOM1 the slowest load drops from 255 to 140 ms and the first tics with monsters awake have no JIT spike even without the warm-up. **The crash T8.3 saw was Godot's .NET shutdown, not ReadyToRun's code:** the runtime's finalizer thread keeps running `GodotObject` finalizers while the engine tears itself down past `main()` (createdump's reports: `GodotObject.Finalize()` of a `Godot.Image` into freed native memory), more often with ReadyToRun's timing (3 in 66 Doom II level checks; 0 in 36 JIT). **Avoided at the root node's exit** (`Main._ExitTree`, the last managed code of a quit, after the music thread stops): `GC.Collect`, the pending finalizers run while the engine is whole, then a 64 MB no-GC region so the teardown queues no more (0 in 80 runs since). An upstream fix (Godot stopping the finalizer thread or draining it in its .NET shutdown) would make it unneeded. |
| 2026-10-15 | **Godot wrappers outlive the native calls that use them (T8.3b).** Godot's generated C# binding hands native code a bare pointer (a `Godot.Collections.Array`'s `NativeValue`, a receiver's `GetPtr`) and keeps no reference to the wrapper (no `GC.KeepAlive`), and the wrappers' finalizers free native data (an `Array`, `Dictionary`, `StringName`, `NodePath`; a `RefCounted` whose wrapper held the last reference). So a wrapper whose last use is a native call can be finalized, on .NET's finalizer thread, during that call: `AddSurfaceFromArrays` then saw a freed array (`p_arrays.size() != ARRAY_MAX`, a sector without a mesh: T8.3b's flake, 3 in 100 loaded Doom II checks), and `GD.Load<PackedScene>(…).Instantiate()` a freed scene. **The rule (decision):** a Godot collection made for one call is `using` (disposed after the call, never finalized); a `RefCounted` only the wrapper keeps alive (a loaded scene not cached elsewhere) is held by a local or a `using` until the call returns, never used inline as a temporary receiver; and a node's `RefCounted` that we update (multimeshes, their meshes) stays in a field of ours rather than being fetched back through a getter, which swaps the wrapper's GC handle and races the finalizer thread inside Godot (`Handle is not initialized`; T8.3d). Implicit `string` → `StringName` arguments stay: the names we pass (actions, shader parameters, methods) are also held by the engine, so finalizing the temporary only drops a reference. `ISODOOM_GC_STRESS=1` (a thread collecting every 2 ms; stopped before `Main._ExitTree`'s no-GC region) makes these races fail at once rather than once in 50 loaded runs. |
| 2026-10-16 | **createdump executable in the Linux export (T8.3c).** Godot's PC export copies the .NET files with `DirAccess::copy` and no mode, after the export plugins have run, so the Linux export's `createdump` lost its exec bit and a crash under `DOTNET_DbgEnableMiniDump=1` wrote no dump. **A `chmod +x` after the export** (the documented command, CI's export step, and the release job, whose artifacts lose modes, which then checks it in the zip) rather than an export plugin (none runs late enough) or leaving it to whoever debugs. The macOS export marks Mach-O files 0755 itself; Windows needs no bit. |
| 2026-10-16 | **No Godot value only a temporary owns is handed to Godot (T8.3d).** The heap corruption (`malloc_consolidate(): unaligned fastbin chunk detected` at a map load, then a hang) was T8.3b's class one level down. A Godot 4.7.2 release template built with symbols (headless, same commit) caught it in `ArrayMesh::add_surface_from_arrays` every time (4 in 120 stressed Doom II checks), with .NET's finalizer thread freeing a `Variant`'s packed array (`PackedArrayRefBase::destroy`) or a `Dictionary` (`Dictionary::_unref`). **The cause:** the binding passes a `Variant` argument as its bare native value (`godot_variant x_in = (godot_variant)x.NativeVar`, no `GC.KeepAlive`), and a non-scalar `Variant` (a string, packed array, object, collection) frees its data in its `Disposer`'s finalizer, so `arrays[(int)Mesh.ArrayType.Vertex] = c.Vertices.ToArray()` (an implicit conversion, then `godotsharp_variant_new_copy`) could have its packed array freed during the copy, whose refcount increment then wrote into a freed fastbin chunk; and for a null optional collection (`AddSurfaceFromArrays`' `blendShapes`, `lods`) the binding makes `new Array<Array>()`/`new Dictionary()` itself and passes them the same way. Collections and `Variant`s left to the finalizer also unreference their objects on the finalizer thread, racing the main thread's unlocked GC-handle swap of the same object in `CSharpLanguage::_instance_binding_reference_callback` (`Handle is not initialized` in `SwapGCHandleForType`, from `InputMap.ActionGetEvents`' arrays; upstream godotengine/godot#112067, open). **The rule (decision), extending T8.3b's:** a `Variant`, a `Godot.Collections` collection or a `RefCounted` made or returned for one native call is disposed on the main thread after its last use (`using`), or held in a field for the run, never left to the finalizer while Godot may use it: `src/Render/GodotRefs.cs` holds the helpers (`SetSlot` for mesh arrays, `AddSurface` with empty blend shapes and LODs of ours, `ActionEvents`, `LoadShader`: loaded shaders cached for the run, as `ResourceCache` holds them by a bare pointer), `StringNames.SetParam` disposes its value, the level script's input goes through `Parse`, the level check holds the events it makes, and a local whose last use is a native call is `using` when it is that object's only owner (a local is dead after its last read in an optimized build: the `ConfigFile`s), but a read-back `Image` or a `SurfaceTool` in a local is kept alive to its scope's end (`GodotRefs.Keep`, `GC.KeepAlive` at the `using`'s end), not disposed: disposed, they came with a corrupt strong GC handle under `ISODOOM_GC_STRESS=1` (the GC faulting or spinning in `Ref_TraceNormalRoots`' handle scan, 7 stressed checks in 450; the dummy renderer's `GetImage` returns the same `Image` it holds on every call), against 0 in 450 without the `using`s; the mechanism wasn't isolated (300,000 disposals of such an image under the stress, alone, stayed clean); `GodotLifetimeTests` (Cecil over the editor build's `IsoDoom.dll`; skips without it) fails on a temporary handed straight to a GodotSharp method, a null collection argument or `ActionGetEvents` outside `GodotRefs` (73 such sites before, 0 after); it can't follow locals, so their rule rests on review. **Measured** (Doom II level checks of the release export, 10 at a time): before, 7 aborts in 100 under `ISODOOM_GC_STRESS=1` (3 `Handle is not initialized`); after, 1 in 300 under the stress (a different fault, a Godot value's destroy on the main thread through a stale pointer at a map load: T8.3f) and 0 aborts or hangs in 100 unstressed (2 over the load budget, 10 checks on 8 cores). |
| 2026-10-16 | **`StringName`s and `NodePath`s are Godot values too (T8.3g).** T8.3b let implicit `string` → `StringName` arguments stay; a review of T8.3d reversed that. `StringName` and `NodePath` free their native data in a finalizer (`StringName.cs`, `NodePath.cs` of GodotSharp), the binding passes them bare (`godot_string_name`, `godot_node_path`) as it does `Variant`s, and `string` → `StringName` is an implicit `new StringName(from)`, so `SetShaderParameter("beam_height", …)`, `AddThemeConstantOverride("separation", …)`, `InputMap.HasAction(action)` or `node.Name = "X"` hand Godot a temporary the finalizer thread may free during the call. The risk is lower than with `Variant`s: names are interned in Godot's global table and the engine usually holds another reference, so the finalizer's destroy mostly only lowers a refcount; but it is the same class of bug and costs nothing to avoid. **The rule (decision):** T8.3d's rule covers `StringName` and `NodePath`: a name passed to Godot goes through `StringNames.Get` (a `StringName` per string, kept for the run; `SetParam` for shader parameters), a `static readonly` field, or a `using` local (the level's `Sector{s}` node names, unbounded, are `using`). `GodotLifetimeScanner` treats `newobj StringName/NodePath::.ctor` and their `op_Implicit` from a `string` as temporaries (103 sites flagged, 0 after; the same limits: an argument with a call of its own after the name stops the follow, so the shader parameters set with `Math.Clamp(…)` values were found by grep), and `GodotLifetimeTests` builds IL against the game's `GodotSharp.dll` to check that it flags them and not a name stored in a field first. Also from the review: T8.3d's `ConfigFile.GetValue` of the IWAD's key without a default printed `Couldn't find the given section "wad" and key "iwad"` at every launch of a player whose `settings.cfg` holds settings but no IWAD; it asks `HasSectionKey` first (the level check reads its own settings file through it with a `Logger` catching engine errors). |

| 2026-10-16 | **M11 multiplayer (the user's decisions; plan `docs/plans/multiplayer.md`).** Online only, each player on their own machine (couch co-op maybe later, as its own plan). Co-op, deathmatch and altdeath with vanilla's rules, at most 4 players (`MAXPLAYERS`). Deterministic lockstep with input delay (no prediction, no rollback) in a star through the host, over Godot's ENet (UDP, direct `address:port`, no relay or NAT traversal). A lobby before the game, no joining in progress, no saves in a netgame, no chat. The fog of war is shared in co-op (the union of every player's walk) and the local player's own in deathmatch. Our own protocol, no vanilla or Chocolate Doom interop, but the sim's netgame rules are checked against vanilla's multi-player demos. The protocol and lockstep code live in a new Godot-free `src/Net`, which feeds the sim nothing but `ticcmd_t[]` |
| 2026-10-16 | **N-player world and the local player (T11.1).** **Players in game** are a mask in the game's settings, `SpawnSettings.playersingame` (bit i: player i + 1; default 1, player 1 alone), not a sibling record: `SpawnSettings` already travels wherever a world is made (saves, the game scene, the tests) and is what T11.5's start message sends; `World`'s constructor sets `playeringame` from it (a mask of 0 or past 4 players throws). Saves write it in their start (`SaveGameFile.VERSION` 5; older saves are refused as older, as for every version). **`consoleplayer`** is a settable `World` property (default 0): a per-machine view index, neither saved nor checksummed, which only picks vanilla's per-machine outputs: the pickup sounds (sim events, never read back), the turbo message (to the console player, taken and cleared by `HU_TakeMessages` in the same tic), and `G_DoCompleted`'s `wminfo.pnum` and `didsecret`, `G_WorldDone`'s `players[consoleplayer].didsecret` (only the intermission reads them, as vanilla's, where they differ per machine too; a player's `didsecret` is saved, but netgames don't save). `idmypos` prints its sender's position (vanilla's console player is the sender there). **`P_SetupLevel`'s `viewz = 1`** is set for every player in the game, not the console player's alone: a deviation behind no tweak because nothing can observe it (the sim never reads `viewz`; `P_CalcHeight` sets it in each player's first `P_PlayerThink`, before any tic's checksum; a reborn player's `G_PlayerReborn` zeroes it anyway; the game loop's menu hold reads it only outside a netgame), so worlds that differ in `consoleplayer` are equal at a level's start too. **Cheats** (our `BTS_CHEAT` in the `ticcmd`, T7.9a) act on the player whose command carries them, `idmus`' and `idclev`'s messages included; still refused in a netgame. **`GameFlow.G_Ticker(ticcmd_t[])`** reads each player in the game's command as vanilla's `G_Ticker`: cheats, the copy into `players[i].cmd` outside the level (the level's is `World.G_Ticker`'s; while paused the level's copy is still skipped, as before), and `BTS_PAUSE`/`BTS_SAVEGAME` from any player; without a world (the title loop) only the console player counts, as vanilla's `playeringame[consoleplayer]`. This machine's special buttons (a save asked for, a cheat typed) go into its own command before it is sent (`GameFlow.G_SpecialButtons`; the single-player `G_Ticker(in ticcmd_t)` calls it, then the array version with the command at `ConsolePlayer` and empty ones for any other player). A `default` argument to `G_Ticker` now binds to the array overload (null): callers pass `new ticcmd_t()`. **Checksum** coverage widened for desync detection: per player the armor type, powers, cards, backpack, frags, weapons owned, maximum ammo, cheats and kill/item/secret counts (the ammo was in since T6.6), and the item respawn queue's head and tail; still nothing per machine (`M_Random`, `consoleplayer`, messages, `didsecret`). Only the coverage changed (the old coverage matched all 191 committed entries; the new one rewrote them). **Test harness** `NetSim` (tests): K worlds of one game, one `consoleplayer` each, fed the same `ticcmd_t[]` a tic, checksums compared every tic and the saved state's bytes at the end. |
| 2026-10-16 | **The lockstep protocol (T11.5; `src/Net`, described in the comment block at the top of `NetMessages.cs`).** A new plain .NET library, `IsoDoom.Net` (no Godot, referenced by the Godot project and the tests; not under the determinism scan, and it hands the sim nothing but `ticcmd_t[]`). **Star through the host:** the host runs a `NetServer` and its own `NetClient`, which takes slot 0 by a host token in its hello; the others take the lowest free slot from 1. **Everything but the pings on the transport's reliable ordered channel** (ENet resends until acknowledged), so the protocol has no acks or resends of its own: lockstep needs every command anyway, and a lost packet costs a resend's delay either way; pings on the unreliable one. **Messages:** one a packet, a type byte and little-endian fields; a `ticcmd_t` is 8 bytes; the hello's type and protocol version come first in every version, so a server refuses another protocol reading only those; a hello with another build stamp (`dev` unstamped matches `dev`), IWAD MD5 or PWAD MD5 list (in order) is refused, as is one to a full game or a game under way. **The start:** the Start carries the settings (`SpawnSettings`, the `Tweaks`, episode, map, `-timer`'s minutes; a test fails when either type gains a field the protocol does not carry), `consoleplayer` (the receiver's slot), the players' mask, the start tic and the input delay; the server fills the first *delay* tics with empty commands. **Tics:** a client sends its command for tic *t* + delay at its clock tic *t*, never more than `BackupTics` (64, vanilla's `BACKUPTICS` is 12: 340 ms, too short for the internet) past the last tic it ran; the server bundles each tic once every player in game sent it, in order, a players' mask per tic (the game's `playeringame`); a client runs tic *t* only from its bundle and only once its clock reached *t*, so an early bundle waits and the input delay holds. **Clock (Chocolate Doom's lag adjustment, our way):** the server's Pong carries its game clock; the client estimates it now from the recent pong with the shortest round trip (8 samples, a ping every 100 ms) plus half that round trip, and runs its clock at 1 + the error in seconds (gain 1/s), within ±5 %; a proportional rule, so a clock 2 % off settles about 20 ms behind or ahead (one tic), measured in the tests; the host's own client is the reference (error 0). **Checksums:** windows of 35 tics from the start tic; after each window a client reports `World.Checksum()`, every player's consistancy (vanilla's: the player mobj's `x`, the `P_Random` index without one, as a `short`) and the first tic whose bundle carried a consistancy differing from its own; each command carries its sender's own consistancy after tic *t* − 64 (vanilla's `G_BuildTiccmd` stamps *maketic*'s), checked by every receiver as vanilla's `G_Ticker`. The server compares a window once every player still in game reported it and announces the first that differs (`Desync`: window, first and last tic) once; stopping the game on it is T11.11's. **Quits:** a quit or a disconnect in game drops the player from the bundles from the first tic it sent no command for (`PlayerQuit` tells everyone; the bundles' masks are what the game applies, as vanilla's `NCMD_EXIT`). **Tests** (`tests/IsoDoom.Tests/Net`) run over `MemoryTransport` (per-endpoint latency, jitter, unreliable loss, reliable resends with head-of-line blocking; seeded): 2–4 players for 3500 tics at 0–100 ms one way (round trips to 270 ms), jitter and loss, with worlds on the synthetic IWAD (player 1 only: its E1M1 has one start) and, with DOOM1.WAD, every player on E1M1 with monsters (god mode: no netgame reborn before T11.3); about 650 B/s up and 800–1400 B/s down a player |

| 2026-10-17 | **The desync tools (T11.11's follow-up).** A desync's checksums say *when* two machines parted, not *what* parted. **The state as text:** `World.DumpState` writes every field `World.Checksum()` hashes, in its order, labelled (`world`, `mobj I` in thinker order with its type, `sector I`, `brain`, `player N`), so two dumps of one tic differ in a line exactly where the checksums' inputs differ; the dumps add `state.txt`, the world the game stopped with (cheap: written once, at the stop). **Replay, not a per-tic dump in play:** a running netgame keeps no per-tic state (it would cost every tic); instead `NetDesyncReplay` (plain C# in `src/Game`, the dev tool `tools/NetDesync`, a console project like RouteGen) loads the agreed save on the same WADs and replays `commands.txt` as the netgame ran it (`GameFlow.RunTic`'s quits by the logged players' mask, `BTS_PAUSE`, the cheats but `idmus`, then `World.G_Ticker`), dumping each tic to `replay.txt` and setting its checksum against the logged one. A machine's replay that reproduces its own checksums shows its run kept to its commands; one that does not names the tic where something else touched its world (a level script's events or `netdesync`, nondeterminism on that machine). For a desync between platforms each machine replays its own dump and `compare.py` compares the two replays field by field. **Limits, accepted:** one level (it stops at an exit or `idclev`: the flow's intermission and next map are not replayed); a pause in force at the save is the flow's, not the save's, so it is not known; the level script's route events (shared script in the loopback tests) are not in the commands, so such runs differ from their replays at those tics (the game's players never run them). **`compare.py`** (Python 3, no packages) lines the logs up by tic over the tics both kept (the first differing checksum or consistancy, the first differing commands, which would be a protocol bug), each replay against its own run, the replays against each other, and the stopped states against each other and against each replay. |

| 2026-10-17 | **Player names and LAN discovery (T11.9c, T11.9d; the user's decisions, `docs/plans/lan-discovery.md`).** **Discovery:** the client queries, the hosts answer (Chocolate Doom's `net_query.c` LAN search): a join sends a small UDP query to the broadcast address on a fixed discovery port and every host listening there answers the sender (not host beacons: two clients on one machine would fight over the port; not mDNS/DNS-SD: too much for a 4-player LAN finder). **The host's lobby page shows its LAN addresses**, for when discovery can't work (guest Wi-Fi, another subnet, the internet). **When:** automatically, the whole time Join Game shows (no Search item). **Hosts a join can't play with** are listed greyed with the reason (another build, IWAD or PWADs, full, started); picking one plays `sfx_oof` and shows the reason back on the page. **IPv4 only** for now (IPv6's `ff02::1` multicast may come later). **Player names** (T11.9c): an option, `net/name` (Multiplayer → PLAYER NAME, typed as Join Game's address; default `""`, the OS user name resolved when used, `Settings.PlayerName`, so the default stays a constant; `PLAYER` when nothing of it is drawable; at most 15 characters, upper case, the menu font's `!`–`_` and spaces), `-name NAME` for a run (pinned, not saved), sent in the hello (protocol 4: the hello's name after the host token; the Lobby message a name per occupied slot in slot order). The server cleans each name (`NetProtocol.CleanName`: upper case, what the font can't draw a `?`, trimmed, 15 characters, an empty one `PLAYER N`); **duplicates are allowed**, the slot colour tells them apart. Names are the lobby's only: nothing in the Start, the bundles or the sim carries them, so no checksum can change, and vanilla's "Player N left the game" stays vanilla's. They show on the lobby page's slot rows (`NAME`, its colour, ping, and `YOU` or `HOST`), in the level script's `lobby` and `net`, the `Net:` log lines and the desync dumps' `summary.txt` (`names:`). The Multiplayer page's cursor starts on Host Game, below the name, so the menus' keys to Host Game stay as they were. |
| 2026-10-17 | **LAN discovery (T11.9d; `docs/plans/lan-discovery.md`).** **Port and format:** discovery has its own UDP port, `NetDiscovery.Port` = 5028 (ENet owns the game port and drops what it doesn't know), our own format (`NetDiscovery.cs`'s comment block; Chocolate Doom's `net_query.c` the idea): a 256-byte query, a reply never bigger (its name, then map, then IWAD name cut), anything else ignored; the PWADs as one MD5 over their MD5s. Plain `System.Net.Sockets`, polled, no threads. **Hosts answer** from their session's start to its end (lobby and game: a started game shows greyed), bound on `-bind`'s address, at most 4 replies a second a source address; no switch to turn it off. A second host on one machine can't bind 5028: it logs `not visible on the LAN` and its lobby page says so; the first is found. **The browser** queries every 1.5 s the limited broadcast, every interface's subnet broadcast (the limited one leaves only by the default interface on some systems) and 127.0.0.1; keeps a game 5 s after its last reply; one row a host (its random instance id), its LAN source address preferred to loopback; sorted by name, then address; at most 6 rows. **Compatibility** in the hello's order (protocol, build, IWAD, PWADs, started, full; `NetIdentity.Refusal`, shared with `NetServer.Hello`), shown short on the row (`ANOTHER BUILD`, `ANOTHER IWAD`, `OTHER PWADS`, `STARTED`, `FULL`; the IWAD's name in parentheses when it fits) and whole on the hint row and in the message. **Join Game:** ADDRESS, CONNECT, `LOCAL GAMES:` (with `SEARCHING...` until a game answers), the games (`NAME` and `E1M1 COOP 2/4`, the mode as `COOP`, `DM` or `ALTDM` to fit the value column), `RECENT:` and the recent addresses (the old `RECENT:` label became a heading item); the cursor follows its item as the rows change. The menus own the browser (made on the page's first frame, disposed when it goes); no page, no LAN traffic. **Greyed text** (the headings, the games one can't join): the menu font through COLORMAP row 16, half as bright (no grey ramp mapping: the font's red stays red, darker). **The host's addresses:** the lobby's first line, `HOSTING ON 192.168.1.23:5029` (the first up, non-loopback, non-link-local IPv4 address, a second when it fits; 127.0.0.1 without one; the bound one for `-bind`). **macOS:** Godot 4.7.2's macOS preset has no option for `NSLocalNetworkUsageDescription`, so `application/additional_plist_content` carries it. **Testing:** `lan [N [SECONDS]] [join]` (join: the first compatible game, as its row would; for `loopback.sh --lan`), and the level check's hosts on free discovery ports with Join Game's browser aimed at them (`LevelScene.DiscoveryPort`, `DiscoveryTargets`); the tests never use 5028 (they run in parallel). |

| 2026-10-19 | **P11: a netgame with a PWAD; the loopback test's PWADs and refusal mode; co-op walked exits.** A netgame on a PWAD set needs nothing of its own in the game: the hello already carries the PWADs' MD5s in load order (T11.6) and a different list or order is refused (`PwadMismatch`), shown on the join page (T11.9). **`loopback.sh` (decision):** PWADs are options before the mode, `--file PWAD` (repeated, in load order: both machines) and `--client-file PWAD` (repeated, `-` for none: the client's own list), rather than a set name, so any files can be tried (another order, a list of another set); a route with a `pwad SET` header loads the set on both machines by itself in `--play`, `--kill` and `--desync` (through `tools/VanillaRef/pwads.py`, the tools' table), so `--play ROUTE NEXT` needs only the IWAD as for the IWAD routes. A new `--refuse` mode checks the refusal as a person meets it: the host waits in its lobby, the client joins through the menus (Multiplayer, Join Game, the address typed) on its own list, and must get the host's `PwadMismatch`, the message over the join page and the join page back after a key; the host is stopped once the client is done. **Co-op routes (decision):** P10's walked exits are single-player, and a netgame's `--play` needs a `players 2` route; RouteGen's walked exit takes a `-2p` suffix (`SET-mapNN-walk-exit-2p`, any walked exit name in fact): both players stand 70 tics, then player 2, the client, walks from its own start to the exit while player 1 stands (as `coop-e1m1-client-exit`: the client's commands drive the game, the harder side of the lockstep), no monsters (the walker plans as if none: P10's routes have none either), no route events, so `loopback.sh` plays it `from=70` and the netgame is the route's game from its first tic. Committed for Scythe MAP01 and DTWID E1M1, each matching vanilla as a netdemo (`PwadRouteTests`). Results in PWAD-VALIDATION.md's P11. Not in CI (WAD-derived): T11.6b runs the PWAD exchange and refusals there on a generated PWAD. |

A piece off its line's span (a connector between two segs' floor edges, which `WallPieces` adds both ways; a floor edge past a tiny line's end, Requiem MAP03) is the map data's. | 2026-10-19 | **T11.12 measurements: home wifi (T11.12e).** T11.12's LAN test (two laptops on home wifi, pings of 5–100 ms) froze often, which the measurements' 5 ms of uniform jitter never showed; `docs/net-freezes-tasks.md` reads why. **The wifi link** (`NetLinks.Wifi`, the remote end's `MemoryLinkOptions`; the server's end perfect, so the one link carries both ways): 2 ms each way and 0–3 ms of jitter (a 4–10 ms round trip); **bursts of spikes** (wifi power saving, another device on the air): one every 1–3 s, each 0.2–0.6 s long, in which every packet takes 25–75 ms more each way (a round trip 50–150 ms more), on each endpoint's own schedule drawn from its seed apart from the packets' randomness, so a later task's other traffic meets the same bursts at the same times; **loss**: 1 % of unreliable packets lost either way; on the reliable channel loss is not dropped data but a **resend**: 1 % of reliable packets either way arrive `ResendUs` = 150 ms late (ENet's retransmit timeout, its smoothed round trip + 4 × the round trip's variance, about 20 + 4 × 30 ms on such a link; a burst's packet may be resent too), and every reliable packet behind it waits (ordered delivery). `MemoryLinkOptions.ReliableLoss` now applies to the packets both ways, as `UnreliableLoss` did (it applied only to what the endpoint sent, so a remote link with a perfect server end never saw a bundle resent); with it two of `LockstepTests`' DOOM1 cases compared the final checksums of worlds that had run different numbers of tics (a player may run past the count while the others catch up; every tic was already compared), so `LockstepHarness.Play` now stops each player's tics at the count. **Stalls:** a stall is the client's own (T11.11's `NetClient.LateUs`: its game clock past the last bundle, the world held), from when it began to the frame its bundle came (a frame's resolution); `StallStats` gives the count, the longest and the time held, each a minute, of the player held longest (the host's own client included: it waits for the others' commands). **Baseline, before T11.12a–d** (`NetMeasurementTests.Stalls_on_home_wifi`, a minute, seeds 1–3; `A_longer_netdelay_on_home_wifi`): 2 players: the measured round trip 18 ms (between bursts), the rule's delay 2 (57 ms): **55–58 stalls a minute, the longest 0.62–0.67 s, 13.8–14.4 s held a minute** (the host 79–104 stalls, the longest 0.22–0.27 s, 5.4–6.7 s held), 473–492 tics late, felt 74–75 ms mean and 301–336 ms worst; 4 players: 35 ms, delay 3 (86 ms): 106 stalls, the longest 0.53 s, 14.8 s held (each of the three remote players about the same). Bandwidth as T11.12's (0.64 KB/s up, 1.04 / 1.56 KB/s down in payload). **`-netdelay`** on the 2-player link (seed 1): 4 → 90 stalls, the longest 230 ms, 5.8 s held; **6 → 9 stalls, 142 ms, 0.8 s held** (felt 172 ms); 8 → 5 stalls, 71 ms, 0.3 s held (felt 229 ms); what is left at 8 is the reliable resends. The internet cases (5 ms jitter, no loss) stall at most once a minute under the rule. T11.12a–d add their numbers here. The player readme's multiplayer section says to turn wifi power saving off or use ethernet, and gives `-netdelay N`. |

## 13. Open questions / risks

1. **Ortho vs perspective camera.** *Closed in T3.3 (§12):* orthographic by default; a 25° perspective camera is a presentation option.
2. **Light diminishing.** *Closed in T3.7 (§12):* vanilla's tables fed the horizontal distance from the player (T2.8), but never less than 80 units (about vanilla's nearest visible floor), so the floor and the player's sprite right around the player are not full bright; no diminishing (reference distance now 128) and camera depth stay presentation options.
3. **Sprite readability.** Doom sprites are drawn for eye-level views. Seen from above at a 45–60° pitch they may look "flat". Mitigations: billboard tilt toward the camera, blob shadows, outlines. *Narrowed in T3.6 (§12):* upright billboards are foreshortened to cos 55° ≈ 57 % of their height like the walls, which made monsters squat and pickups flat; fully tilted ones (with an upright billboard's depth) show the patches as drawn and read clearly on E1M1 and Doom II MAP01, and a black outline separates them from dark floors. Default: full tilt, upright depth, black outline, no shadows. *Still open:* look again with moving and attacking monsters, flying ones (lost souls, cacodemons: a shadow on the floor below would show their height; the shadow sits at the thing's z for now) and projectiles in M4/M5.
4. **Tall-room readability.** Very high walls (E1M8, Doom II city maps) may need a lower cutaway threshold or per-area camera tweaks. *Narrowed in T3.4 (§12):* the cutaway is measured from the player's floor, not the room's height, so the player stays visible behind walls of any height (E1M8 and Doom II MAP15 toured by script: the placeholder at least 30 % and 40 % visible, the lowest where it straddles a wall it walked into); what remains is how much of the room around the player tall walls hide outside the 80-unit disc (radius and zoom are options). Look again in T3.8 with things and monsters on the map.
5. **Aim on gamepad / Steam Deck.** Right-stick aiming loses cursor distance. Aim assist may need to be stronger on a pad, possibly with a lock-on toggle. Test on a Deck from M6 onward. *Narrowed in T6.7 (§12):* the strength is `Tweaks.AimAssistCone` (±5° by default, `--level-aim-assist`), counted from a target's edge; tuning on a pad is T8.2.
6. **Determinism cost.** The fixed-point-only sim makes the presentation layer convert every frame, and it rules out Godot physics for gameplay. Track this; if it becomes painful, revisit the co-op goal.
